2.7
Discussion and Future Work

This paper presents a new approach for improving estimation of the explained variance
\tau^{2}
of a high-dimensional regression model in a semi-supervised setting without assuming sparsity. The key idea is to use a zero-estimator that is correlated with the initial unbiased estimator of
\tau^{2}
in order to lower its variance without introducing additional bias. The semi-supervised setting, where the number of observations is much greater than the number of responses, allows us to construct such zero-estimators. We introduced a new notion of optimality with respect to zero-estimators and presented an oracle-estimator that achieves this type of optimality.
We proposed two different (non-oracle) estimators that showed
a significant reduction, but not optimal, in the asymptotic variance of the naive estimator.
Our simulations showed that our approach can be generalized to other types of initial estimators other than the naive estimator.

Many open questions remain for future research.
While our proposed estimators improved the naive estimator, it did not achieve the optimal improvement of the oracle estimator.
Thus, it remains unclear if and how one can achieve optimal improvement.
Moreover, the theory developed in this work relies on a strong assumption about unsupervised data size, i.e.,
N=\infty
.
Thus, generalizing our suggested theory by relaxing this assumption to allow for a general setting with a
finite

N\gg n
is a natural direction for future work.
A more ambitious future goal would be to extend the suggested approach to generalized linear models (GLM), and specifically to logistic regression. In this case, the concepts of signal and noise levels are less clear and are more challenging to define.

Supplementary Material

Proof of Lemma
2.1
:

Notice that
{{\textbf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}={\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{W_{i1}}},...,\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{W_{ip}}}}\right)^{T}}
where
X
is the
n\times p
design matrix and
\textbf{Y}=(Y_{1},...,Y_{n})^{T}
.
Thus, the naive estimator can be also written as

\hat{\tau}^{2}=\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{W_{{i_{1}}j}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}=}}\frac{{{{\left\|{{{\textbf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}}}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}.

The Dicker estimate for
\tau^{2}
ia given by

\hat{\tau}^{2}_{Dicker}\equiv\frac{{{{\left\|{{{\textbf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}-p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}}}{{n\left({n+1}\right)}}.

We need to prove that root-
n
times the difference between the estimators converges in probability to zero, i.e.,
\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\tau}^{2}_{Dicker}-\hat{\tau}^{2}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0
.
We have,

\begin{split}\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\tau}^{2}_{Dicker}-\hat{\tau}^{2}\right)&=\sqrt{n}\left({\frac{{{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}-p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}}}{{n\left({n+1}\right)}}-\frac{{{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}}}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}}\right)\\
&=\sqrt{n}\left({\frac{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}}}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}-\frac{{p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}}}{{n\left({n+1}\right)}}-\frac{{2{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)\left({n+1}\right)}}}\right).\end{split}

(2.23)

It is enough to prove that:

1.

n^{-1.5}\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}-p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0,

2.

n^{-2.5}\left({{{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.

We start with the first term,

n^{-1.5}\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}-p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}\right)=n^{-1.5}\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{Y_{i}^{2}X_{ij}^{2}}}-p\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{Y_{i}^{2}}}\right)\\
=n^{-1.5}\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{Y_{i}^{2}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{X_{ij}^{2}}}-p\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{Y_{i}^{2}}}\right)=n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{Y_{i}^{2}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\left({X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right)}}\right]\equiv n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}

(2.24)

where
{\omega_{i}}=Y_{i}^{2}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j}{\left\{{X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right\}}}\right].
Notice that
\omega_{i}
depends on
n
but this is suppressed in the notation. In order to show that
n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0,
it is enough to show that
E\left({n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}}\right)\to 0
and
\text{Var}\left({n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}}\right)\to 0.

Moreover, since
E\left({n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}}\right)=\sqrt{n}E\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)
and
\text{Var}\left({n^{-0.5}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}}\right)=\text{Var}\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)=E\left({\omega_{i}^{2}}\right)-{\left[{E\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)}\right]^{2}},
it is enough to show that
\sqrt{n}E\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)
and
E\left({\omega_{i}^{2}}\right)
converge to zero.

Consider now
\sqrt{n}E\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)
.
By (
2.24
) we have

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{\omega_{i}}}=\frac{1}{n}\left[{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}}-p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right].

Taking expectation of both sides,

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{E\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{n}\left[{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{E\left({W_{ij}^{2}}\right)}}-pE\left({{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)}\right].

Now, notice that

E(W_{ij}^{2})=E[X_{ij}^{2}(\beta^{T}X+\varepsilon)^{2}]={\left\|\beta\right\|^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}+\beta_{j}^{2}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]={\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}+2\beta_{j}^{2}.

(2.25)

Also notice that
Y_{i}^{2}/\left({\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+{\tau^{2}}}\right)\sim\chi_{1}^{2},
and hence
E\left({{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)=n\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle nE\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left[{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}+2\beta_{j}^{2}}\right)}}-pn\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left[{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left[{p\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)+2{\tau^{2}}}\right]-pn\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)}}\right]=2{\tau^{2}}

which implies that
\sqrt{n}E\left({{\omega_{i}}}\right)=\frac{{2{\tau^{2}}}}{{\sqrt{n}}}\overset{}{\rightarrow}~0.

Consider now
E(\omega_{i}^{2})
.
By Cauchy-Schwartz,

E\left({\omega_{i}^{2}}\right)=E\left({Y_{i}^{4}{{\left[{n^{-1}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\left\{{X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right\}}}\right]}^{2}}}\right)\leq{\left\{{E\left({Y_{i}^{8}}\right)}\right\}^{1/2}}{\left\{{E\left({{{\left[{n^{-1}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\left\{{X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right\}}}\right]}^{4}}}\right)}\right\}^{1/2}}.

Notice that
{Y_{i}}\sim N\left({0,{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)
by construction and therefore
E(Y_{i}^{8})=O(1)
as
n
and
p
go to infinity.
Let
V_{j}=X_{ij}^{2}-1
and notice that
E(V_{j})=0.
We have

E\left({{{\left[{{n^{-1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\left\{{X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right\}}}\right]}^{4}}}\right)=E\left({{{\left[{{n^{-1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{V_{j}}}}\right]}^{4}}}\right)={n^{-4}}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{E\left({{V_{{j_{1}}}}{V_{{j_{2}}}}{V_{{j_{3}}}}{V_{{j_{4}}}}}\right)}.

The expectation

\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{E\left({{V_{{j_{1}}}}{V_{{j_{2}}}}{V_{{j_{3}}}}{V_{{j_{4}}}}}\right)}

is not 0 when
j_{1}=j_{2}
and
j_{3}=j_{4}
(up to permutations) or when all terms are equal. In the first case we have

\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{E\left({V_{j}^{2}V_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)=}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{{\left[{E\left({V_{j}^{2}}\right)}\right]}^{2}}=}p\left({p-1}\right){\left[{E\left\{{{{\left({X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right)}^{2}}}\right\}}\right]^{2}}\leq C_{1}p^{2},

for a positive constant
C_{1}.

In the second case we have

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({V_{j}^{4}}\right)=}pE\left[{{{\left({X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right)}^{4}}}\right]\leq C_{2}p,
for a positive constant
C_{2}
.
Hence, as
p
and
n
have the same order of magnitude, we have

\displaystyle{\left\{{E\left[{{{\left({{n^{-1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\left\{{X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right\}}}\right)}^{4}}}\right]}\right\}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle={\left\{{{n^{-4}}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{E\left({{V_{{j_{1}}}}{V_{{j_{2}}}}{V_{{j_{3}}}}{V_{{j_{4}}}}}\right)}}\right\}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq{\left\{{{n^{-4}}\cdot O\left({{p^{2}}}\right)}\right\}^{1/2}}\leq K/n,

which implies
E(\omega_{i}^{2})\leq K_{1}/n\rightarrow 0,
where
K
and
K_{1}
are positive constants.
This completes the proof that

n^{-1.5}\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{W_{ij}^{2}}-p{{\left\|{\bf{Y}}\right\|}^{2}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0.

We now move to prove that

n^{-2.5}\left({{{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.

By Markov’s inequality, for
\varepsilon>0

P\left({{n^{-2.5}}{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}>\varepsilon}\right)\leq{n^{-2.5}}E\left({{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)/\varepsilon.

Thus, it is enough to show that
{n^{-2}}E\left({{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)
is bounded.
Notice that

\begin{split}E\left({{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)&=\sum\limits_{{i_{1}},{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}}\right)}}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({W_{ij}^{2}}\right)}}+\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}}\right)}}\\
&=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}+2\beta_{j}^{2}}\right)}}+\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}}}=n\left[{p\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)+2{\tau^{2}}}\right]+n\left({n-1}\right){\tau^{2}}\\
&=n\left[{p\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)+\left({n+1}\right){\tau^{2}}}\right],\end{split}

where we used (
2.25
) in the third equality.
Therefore,

{n^{-2}}E\left({{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)={n^{-1}}\left[{p\left({{\tau^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}}\right)+\left({n+1}\right){\tau^{2}}}\right].

Since
p
and
n
have the same order of magnitude and
\tau^{2}
+
\sigma^{2}
is bounded by assumption, then

{n^{-2}}E\left({{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}\right)
is also bounded. This completes the proof of

n^{-2.5}\left({{{{\left\|{{{\bf{X}}^{T}}{\bf{Y}}}\right\|}^{2}}}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0

and hence
\sqrt{n}\left({\hat{\tau}_{Dicker}^{2}-{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0.

∎

Proof of Corollary
2.1
:

According to Corollary 1 in
Dicker 2014
, we have

\frac{{\sqrt{n}\left({{\hat{\tau}_{Dicker}}-{\tau^{2}}}\right)}}{\psi}\overset{D}{\rightarrow}N\left({0,1}\right),

where

\psi=2\left\{{\left({1+\frac{p}{n}}\right){{\left({\sigma^{2}+{\tau^{2}}}\right)}^{2}}-\sigma^{4}+3{\tau^{4}}}\right\},
given that
p/n
converges to a constant.
Therefore we can write

\frac{{\sqrt{n}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-{\tau^{2}}}\right)}}{\psi}=\frac{1}{\psi}\left[{\sqrt{n}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-{{\hat{\tau}}_{Dicker}}}\right)+\sqrt{n}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}_{Dicker}}-{\tau^{2}}}\right)}\right],

and obtain
\sqrt{n}\left({\frac{{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-{\tau^{2}}}}{\psi}}\right)\overset{D}{\rightarrow}N(0,1)\,
by Slutsky’s theorem. ∎

Proof of Proposition
2.1
:

Let
{{\bf{W}}_{i}}={\left({{W_{i1}},...,{W_{ip}}}\right)^{T}}
and notice that
\hat{\tau}^{2}=\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}}
is a U-statistic of order 2 with the kernel
h\left({{{\bf{w}}_{1}},{{\bf{w}}_{2}}}\right)={\bf{w}}_{1}^{T}{{\bf{w}}_{2}}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{w_{1j}}{w_{2j}}}
, where
{{\bf{w}}_{i}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
.

By Theorem 12.3 in
van der Vaart 2000
,

{\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{4\left({n-2}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}{\zeta_{1}}+\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}{\zeta_{2}},

(2.26)

where
{\zeta_{1}}={\text{Cov}}\left[{h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\bf{W}}_{2}}}\right),h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\widetilde{\bf{{W}}}_{2}}}}\right)}\right]

and

{\zeta_{2}}={\text{Cov}}\left[{h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\bf{W}}_{2}}}\right),h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\bf{W}}_{2}}}\right)}\right]

where
\widetilde{\bf{{W}}}_{2}
is an independent copy of
\bf{W}_{2}
.
Now, let
{\bf{A}}=E\left({{{\bf{W}}_{i}}{\bf{W}}_{i}^{T}}\right)
be a
p\times p
matrix and notice that

\displaystyle{\zeta_{1}}

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left[{h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\bf{W}}_{2}}}\right),h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\widetilde{\bf{W}}}_{2}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}^{p}{\text{Cov}\left({{W_{1j}}{W_{2j}},{W_{1j^{\prime}}}{{\widetilde{W}}_{2j^{\prime}}}}\right)}=\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}^{p}{\left({{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left[{{W_{1j}}{W_{1j^{\prime}}}}\right]-\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle={\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}

and

\displaystyle{\zeta_{2}}

\displaystyle={\text{Cov}}\left[{h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\bf{W}}_{2}}}\right),h\left({{{\bf{W}}_{1}},{{\bf{W}}_{2}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{W_{1j}}{W_{2j}},{W_{1j^{\prime}}}{W_{2j^{\prime}}}}\right)}=\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}\left({{{\left({E\left[{{W_{1j}}{W_{1j^{\prime}}}}\right]}\right)}^{2}}-\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}},

where
\|\textbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}
is the Frobenius norm of
\textbf{A}.
Thus, by rewriting (
2.26
) the variance of the naive estimator is given by

{\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{4\left({n-2}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{{\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{{\left\|\beta\right\|}^{4}}}\right]+\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{{\left\|\beta\right\|}^{4}}}\right].

(2.27)

Proof of Proposition
2.2
:

Notice that
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is consistent if
\text{Var}[\hat{\tau}^{2}]\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
since
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is unbiased.
Thus, by (
2.27
) it is enough to require that
\frac{\beta^{T}\textbf{A}\beta}{n}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
and
\frac{\|\textbf{A}\|^{2}_{F}}{n^{2}}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
. The latter is assumed and we now show that the former also holds true.
Let
\lambda_{1}\geq...\geq\lambda_{p}
be the eigenvalues of
A

and notice that
A
is symmetric.
We have that
n^{-2}\lambda_{1}^{2}\leq n^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}^{2}=n^{-2}tr(\textbf{A}^{2})=n^{-2}\|\textbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}
and therefore (iii) implies that
\frac{\lambda_{1}}{n}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
.
Now,

\frac{1}{n}\beta^{T}\textbf{A}\beta\equiv\frac{1}{n}\|\beta\|^{2}[(\frac{\beta}{\|\beta\|})^{T}\textbf{A}\frac{\beta}{\|\beta\|}]\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\beta\|^{2}\lambda_{1}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0,
where the last limit follows from the assumption that
\tau^{2}=O(1),
and from the fact that
\frac{\lambda_{1}}{n}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
. We conclude that
\text{Var}[\hat{\tau}^{2}]\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
.

We now prove the moreover part, that is, independence of the columns of
X
implies that

\frac{\|\textbf{A}\|^{2}_{F}}{n^{2}}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
.
By definition we have

\|\textbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}[E(W_{ij}W_{ij^{\prime}})]^{2}.

Notice that when
j=j^{\prime}
we have,

\displaystyle E\left({W_{ij}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left({X_{ij}^{2}Y_{i}^{2}}\right)=E\left({X_{ij}^{2}{{\left[{{\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}+{\varepsilon_{i}}}\right]}^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left({X_{ij}^{2}\left[{\sum\limits_{k,k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{k^{\prime}}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}+2{\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}}+\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left({X_{ij}^{2}\sum\limits_{k,k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{k^{\prime}}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}}\right)+0+E\left({X_{ij}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left({X_{ij}^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{p}{\beta_{k}^{2}X_{ik}^{2}}}\right)+\underbrace{E\left({X_{ij}^{2}\sum\limits_{k\neq k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{k^{\prime}}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}}\right)}_{0}+\sigma^{2}E\left({X_{ij}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{j}^{2}E\left({X_{ij}^{4}}\right)+\sum\limits_{k\neq j}^{p}{\beta_{k}^{2}\underbrace{E\left({X_{ik}^{2}X_{ij}^{2}}\right)}_{1}}+{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{j}^{2}E\left({X_{ij}^{4}}\right)+{\left\|\beta\right\|^{2}}-\beta_{j}^{2}+{\sigma^{2}}={\left\|\beta\right\|^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}+\beta_{j}^{2}\left[{E\left({X_{ij}^{4}-1}\right)}\right].

Notice that
E\left({X_{ij}^{2}\sum\limits_{k\neq k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{k^{\prime}}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}}\right)=0
follows from the assumptions that the columns of
{\bf X}
are independent and
E(X_{ij})=0
for each
j
. Also notice that in the third row we used the assumption that
E(\varepsilon_{i}^{2}|X_{i})=\sigma^{2}.

Similarly, when
j\neq j^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle E\left({{W_{ij}}{W_{ij}}}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left({{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}Y_{i}^{2}}\right)=E\left[{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}{{\left({{\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}+{\varepsilon_{i}}}\right)}^{2}}}\right]=E\left[{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}{{\left({{\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}+{\varepsilon_{i}}}\right)}^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}\left({\sum\limits_{k,k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{k^{\prime}}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}+2{\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}}+\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}\sum\limits_{k,k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{k}}{\beta_{k^{\prime}}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}}\right]+0+E\left({{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left({X_{ij}^{2}X_{ij^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)+0+\underbrace{E\left({{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}\right)}_{0}E\left({\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}\right)=2{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left({X_{ij}^{2}}\right)E\left({X_{ij^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}.

This can be written more compactly as

E(W_{ij}W_{ij^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}2{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}},&j\neq j^{\prime}\\
\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{j}^{2}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1],&j=j^{\prime},\end{cases}

(2.28)

where
\sigma_{Y}^{2}={\left\|\beta\right\|^{2}}+{\sigma^{2}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\textbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{j}\Big(\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{j}^{2}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\beta\|^{4}+\sum_{j}\Big(\sigma_{Y}^{4}+\beta_{j}^{4}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]^{2}+2\sigma_{Y}^{2}\beta_{j}^{2}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]\Big)

\displaystyle=p\sigma_{Y}^{4}+O(1).

(2.29)

where the last equality holds since

\sigma_{Y}^{2}\equiv\tau^{2}+\sigma^{2}=O(1)
,

E(X_{ij}^{4})=O(1)
and by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we have
\sum_{j}\beta_{j}^{4}\leq\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}=\|\beta\|^{4}=O(1).

Now since
p/n=O(1)
then
\frac{\|\textbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}}{n^{2}}\rightarrow 0
and we conclude that
\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})=O(\frac{1}{n}),
i.e.,
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is
\sqrt{n}
-consistent.

Remark 2.3
.

Calculations for Example
2.1
:

\begin{split}\text{Cov}[\hat{\tau}^{2},g(X)]&\equiv\text{Cov}\left({\frac{2}{n\left({n-1}\right)}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}{{W_{{i_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}}}},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[X_{i}^{2}-1]}\right)\\
&=\frac{2}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\text{Cov}\left({{X_{{i_{1}}}}{Y_{{i_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{2}}}}{Y_{{i_{2}}}},{X_{i}^{2}}}\right)}}\\
&=\frac{2}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}[{E\left({{X_{{i_{1}}}}{Y_{{i_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{2}}}}{Y_{{i_{2}}}}{X_{i}^{2}})-\beta^{2}}\right]}}\\
&=\frac{4}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}[{E\left({X_{{i_{1}}}^{3}{Y_{{i_{1}}}}}\right)}\beta-\beta^{2}]\\
&=\frac{{4\beta}}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}[{E\left({X_{{i_{1}}}^{3}{Y_{{i_{1}}}}}\right)-\beta]}\\
&=\frac{{4\beta}}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\frac{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}{2}[E\left({X_{{i_{1}}}^{3}{Y_{{i_{1}}}}}\right)-\beta]\\
&=\frac{2\beta}{n}[E\left({{X^{3}}Y}\right)-\beta],\end{split}

(2.30)

where in the third equality we used
E(X^{2})=1
and
E(XY)\equiv\beta.

In the fourth equality the expectation is zero for all
i\neq i_{1},i_{2}
.
Now, since
X\sim N(0,1)
and
E(\varepsilon|X)=0
, then

E\left({{X^{3}}Y}\right)=E\left({{X^{3}}\left({\beta X+\varepsilon}\right)}\right)=\beta E\left({{X^{4}}}\right)=3\beta.

Therefore,

\text{Cov}[{\hat{\beta}{}^{2},{{g}}(X)}]=\frac{4\beta^{2}}{n}.

Notice that

{\mathop{\text{Var}}}\left[{g}\right]={\mathop{\rm var}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left({X_{i}^{2}-1}\right)}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\left[{E\left({{X^{4}}}\right)-E\left({{X^{2}}}\right)}\right]=\frac{2}{n}.

Therefore, by (
2.8
) we get
c^{*}=-2\beta^{2}.

Plugging-in
c^{*}
back in (
2.3.1
) yields
\text{Var}(U_{c^{*}})=\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})-~\frac{8}{n}\beta^{4}.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
:

1. We now prove the first direction: OOE
\Rightarrow\text{Cov}[R^{*},g]=0
for all
g\in\cal G.

Let
R^{*}\equiv T+g^{*}
be an OOE for
\theta
with respect to the family of zero-estimators
\cal G
. By definition,
\text{Var}[R^{*}]\leqslant\text{Var}[T+g]
for all
g\in\cal G
. For every
g=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{k}g_{k}
, define
\tilde{g}\equiv g-g^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}(c_{k}-c_{k}^{*})g_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{c}_{k}g_{k}
for some fixed
m,
and note that
\tilde{g}\in\cal G
. Then,

\begin{split}\text{Var}[R^{*}]&\leqslant\text{Var}[T+g]=\text{Var}[T+g^{*}+\tilde{g}]=\text{Var}[R^{*}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{c}_{k}g_{k}]\\
&=\text{Var}[R^{*}]+2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{c}_{k}\cdot\text{Cov}[R^{*},g_{k}]+\text{Var}[\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{c}_{k}g_{k}].\end{split}

Therefore, for all
(\tilde{c}_{1},...,\tilde{c}_{m})
,

0\leqslant 2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{c_{k}}\cdot\text{Cov}[R^{*},g_{k}]+\text{Var}[\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{c_{k}}g_{k}],

which can be represented compactly as

0\leqslant-2\mathbf{\tilde{c}}^{T}\mathbf{b}+\text{Var}[\mathbf{\tilde{c}}^{T}\mathbf{g_{m}}]=-2\mathbf{\tilde{c}}^{T}\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{\tilde{c}}^{T}M\mathbf{\tilde{c}}\equiv f(\mathbf{\tilde{c}}),

(2.31)

where
\mathbf{b}\equiv-\left(\text{Cov}[R^{*},g_{1}],...,\text{Cov}[R^{*},g_{m}]\right)^{T}
,
\mathbf{g_{m}}\equiv(g_{1},...,g_{m})^{T}
,
M=\text{Cov}[\mathbf{g_{m}}]
and
\mathbf{\tilde{c}}\equiv(\tilde{c}_{1},...,\tilde{c}_{m})^{T}.

Notice that
f(\mathbf{\tilde{c}})
is a convex function in
\mathbf{\tilde{c}}
that satisfies
f(\mathbf{\tilde{c}})\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{\tilde{c}}
.
Differentiate
f(\mathbf{\tilde{c}})
in order to find its minimum

\nabla f(\mathbf{\tilde{c}})=-2\mathbf{b}+2M\mathbf{\tilde{c}}=0.

Assuming
M
is positive definite and solving for
\mathbf{\tilde{c}}
yields the minimizer
\mathbf{\tilde{c}_{min}}=M^{-1}\mathbf{b}
.
Plug-in
\mathbf{\tilde{c}_{min}}
in the (
2.31
) yields

\begin{split}f(\mathbf{\tilde{c}_{min}})\equiv-2(M^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{T}\mathbf{b}+(M^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{T}M(M^{-1}\mathbf{b})=-\mathbf{b}^{T}M^{-1}\mathbf{b}&\geq 0.\end{split}

(2.32)

Since, by assumption,
M
is positive definite, so is
M^{-1}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{b}^{T}M^{-1}\mathbf{b}>0
for all non-zero
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Thus, (
2.32
) is satisfied only if
\mathbf{b}\equiv\mathbf{0}
, i.e.,
\text{Cov}[R^{*},\mathbf{g_{m}}]=\mathbf{0}
which also implies
\text{Cov}[R^{*},\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{k}g_{k}]=0
for any
c_{1},...,c_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore,
\text{Cov}[R^{*},g]=0
for all
g\in\cal G
.

2. We now prove the other direction: if
R^{*}
is uncorrelated with all zero-estimators of a given family
\cal G
then it is an OOE.

Let
R^{*}=T+g^{*}
and
R\equiv T+g
be unbiased estimators of
\theta
, where
g^{*},g\in\cal G
.
Define
\tilde{g}\equiv R^{*}-R=g^{*}-g
and notice that
\tilde{g}\in\cal G
. Since by assumption
R^{*}
is uncorrelated with
\tilde{g}
,

\begin{split}0=\text{Cov}[R^{*},\tilde{g}]\equiv\text{Cov}[R^{*},R^{*}-R]=\text{Var}[R^{*}]-\text{Cov}[R^{*},R],\end{split}

and hence
\text{Var}[R^{*}]=\text{Cov}[R^{*},R]
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

(\text{Cov}[R^{*},R])^{2}\leq\text{Var}[R^{*}]\text{Var}[R],

we conclude that
\text{Var}[R^{*}]\leq\text{Var}[R]=\text{Var}[T+g]
for all
g\in\cal G
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2
:

We start by proving Theorem
2.2
for the special case of
p=2
and then generalize for
p>2
.
By Theorem
2.1
we need to show that
\text{Cov}\left({{T_{oracle}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)=0
for all
\left({{k_{1}},{k_{2}}}\right)\in{\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}}

where
{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left[{X_{i1}^{{k_{1}}}X_{i2}^{{k_{2}}}-E\left({X_{i1}^{{k_{1}}}X_{i2}^{{k_{2}}}}\right)}\right]}
.
Write,

\displaystyle\text{Cov}\left({{T_{oracle}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)-2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{\text{Cov}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)}}.

Thus, we need to show that

\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)=2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{\text{Cov}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)}}.

(2.33)

We start with calculating the LHS of (
2.33
), namely
\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)
. Recall that
\hat{\tau}^{2}\equiv\hat{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\hat{\beta}_{2}^{2}
and therefore
\text{Cov}[\hat{\tau}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}}]=\text{Cov}(\hat{\beta}_{1}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}})+\text{Cov}(\hat{\beta}_{2}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}}).

Now, for all
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
we have

\begin{split}\text{Cov}[\hat{\beta}_{1}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}}]&\equiv\text{Cov}\left(\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}W_{i_{1}1}W_{i_{2}1},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}}-E[X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}}])\right)\\
&=\frac{2}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\text{Cov}\left(X_{i_{1}1}Y_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}1}Y_{i_{2}},X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{2}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(E[X_{i_{1}1}Y_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}1}Y_{i_{2}}X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}}]-\beta_{1}^{2}E[X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}}]\right)\\
&=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}\left(E[X_{i_{1}1}Y_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}1}Y_{i_{2}}X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]-\beta_{1}^{2}E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]\right)\\
&=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}\left(E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}+1}Y_{i_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]E[X_{i_{2}1}Y_{i_{2}}]-\beta_{1}^{2}E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]\right)\\
&=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}\left(E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}+1}Y_{i_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]\beta_{1}-\beta_{1}^{2}E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]\right)\\
&=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n-1)}\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\left(E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+1}Y_{1}X_{12}^{k_{2}}]\beta_{1}-\beta_{1}^{2}E[X_{11}^{k_{1}}X_{12}^{k_{2}}]\right)\\
&=\frac{2}{n}\left(E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+1}Y_{1}X_{12}^{k_{2}}]\beta_{1}-\beta_{1}^{2}E[X_{11}^{k_{1}}X_{12}^{k_{2}}]\right),\end{split}

(2.34)

where the calculations can be justified by similar arguments to those presented in (
2.30
).
We shall use the following notation:

\begin{split}A&\equiv E\left[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}+2}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}}}\right]\\
B&\equiv E\left[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}+1}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}+1}}\right]\\
C&\equiv E\left[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}}}\right]\\
D&\equiv E\left[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}+2}}\right].\end{split}

Notice that
A,B,C
and
D
are functions of
(k_{1},k_{2})
but this is suppressed in the notation.
Write,

\begin{split}E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+1}X_{12}^{k_{2}}Y_{1}]&=E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+1}X_{12}^{k_{2}}(\beta_{1}X_{11}+\beta_{2}X_{12}+\varepsilon_{1})]\\
&=\beta_{1}E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+2}X_{12}^{k_{2}}]+\beta_{2}E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+1}X_{12}^{k_{2}+1}]=\beta_{1}A+\beta_{2}B.\end{split}

Thus, rewrite (
2.34
) and obtain

\text{Cov}[\hat{\beta}_{1}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}}]=\frac{2}{n}\left({\left[{{\beta_{1}}A+{\beta_{2}}B}\right]{\beta_{1}}-\beta_{1}^{2}C}\right).

(2.35)

Similarly, by symmetry,

\text{Cov}[\hat{\beta}_{2}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}}]=\frac{2}{n}\left({\left[{{\beta_{2}}D+{\beta_{1}}B}\right]{\beta_{2}}-\beta_{2}^{2}C}\right).

(2.36)

Using (
2.35
) and (
2.36
) we get

\begin{split}\text{Cov}[\hat{\tau}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}}]&=\text{Cov}(\hat{\beta}_{1}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}})+\text{Cov}(\hat{\beta}_{2}^{2},g_{k_{1}k_{2}})\\
&=\frac{2}{n}\left({\left[{{\beta_{1}}A+{\beta_{2}}B}\right]{\beta_{1}}-\beta_{1}^{2}C+\left[{{\beta_{2}}D+{\beta_{1}}B}\right]{\beta_{2}}-\beta_{2}^{2}C}\right)\\
&=\frac{2}{n}\left[{\overbrace{\beta_{1}^{2}A+\beta_{2}^{2}D}^{{L_{1}}}+\overbrace{2{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{2}}B}^{{L_{2}}}-\overbrace{C\left({\beta_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2}^{2}}\right)}^{{L_{3}}}}\right]=\frac{2}{n}\left({{L_{1}}+{L_{2}}-{L_{3}}}\right).\end{split}

(2.37)

We now move to calculate the RHS of (
2.33
), namely

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)}}.

First, recall that
{h_{jj}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left[{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}-E\left({{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}\right)}\right]}
and
{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left[{X_{i1}^{{k_{1}}}X_{i2}^{{k_{2}}}-E\left({X_{i1}^{{k_{1}}}X_{i2}^{{k_{2}}}}\right)}\right]}
where
\left({{k_{1}},{k_{2}}}\right)\in~\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
. Hence,

{h_{11}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left({X_{i1}^{2}-1}\right)}
which by definition is also equal to
{g_{20}}.
Similarly, we have

{h_{12}}={h_{21}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left({{X_{i1}}{X_{i2}}}\right)={g_{11}}}

and
{h_{22}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left({X_{i2}^{2}-1}\right)={g_{02}}}.

Thus,

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)}}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)}}\\
=\beta_{1}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{11}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)+2{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{2}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{12}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)+\beta_{2}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{22}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)\\
=\beta_{1}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{20}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)+2{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{2}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{11}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)+\beta_{2}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{02}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right).

(2.38)

Now, observe that for every
(k_{1},k_{2},d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4},

\begin{split}\text{Cov}[g_{k_{1}k_{2}},g_{d_{1}d_{2}}]&=\text{Cov}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}}-E(X_{i1}^{k_{1}}X_{i2}^{k_{2}})],\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[X_{i1}^{d_{1}}X_{i2}^{d_{2}}-E(X_{i1}^{d_{1}}X_{i2}^{d_{2}})]\Big)\\
&=n^{-2}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{n}(E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}X_{i_{2}1}^{d_{1}}X_{i_{2}2}^{d_{2}}]-E[X_{i_{1}1}^{k_{1}}X_{i_{1}2}^{k_{2}}]E[X_{i_{2}1}^{d_{1}}X_{i_{2}2}^{d_{2}}])\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\Big(E[X_{11}^{k_{1}+d_{1}}X_{12}^{k_{2}+d_{2}}]-E[X_{11}^{k_{1}}X_{12}^{k_{2}}]E[X_{11}^{d_{1}}X_{12}^{d_{2}}]\Big),\end{split}

(2.39)

where the third equality holds since the terms with
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
vanish.
It follows from (
2.39
) that

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Cov}[g_{k_{1}k_{2}},g_{20}]=\frac{1}{n}\left(E[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}+2}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}}}}]-E[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}}}]\right)=\frac{1}{n}(A-C),\\
\text{Cov}[g_{k_{1}k_{2}},g_{11}]=\frac{1}{n}E[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}+1}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}+1}}]=\frac{B}{n},\\
\text{Cov}[g_{k_{1}k_{2}},g_{02}]=\frac{1}{n}\left(E[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}+2}}]-E[{X_{11}^{{k_{1}}}}{X_{12}^{{k_{2}}}}]\right)=\frac{1}{n}(D-C)\\
\end{array}

Therefore, rewrite (
2.38
) to get

\displaystyle 2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{2}{\text{Cov}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}{k_{2}}}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\left[{\overbrace{\beta_{1}^{2}A+\beta_{2}^{2}D}^{{L_{1}}}+\overbrace{2{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{2}}B}^{{L_{2}}}-\overbrace{C\left({\beta_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2}^{2}}\right)}^{{L_{3}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\left({{L_{1}}+{L_{2}}-{L_{3}}}\right)

(2.40)

which is exactly the same expression as in (
2.37
).
Hence, equation (
2.33
) follows which completes the proof of Theorm
2.2
for
p=2.

We now generalize the proof for
p>2
.
Similarly to (
2.33
) we want to show that

\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)=2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{\text{Cov}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}}.

(2.41)

We begin by calculating the LHS of (
2.41
), i.e., the covariance between
\hat{\tau}^{2}
and
g_{k_{1}...k_{p}}.

By the same type of calculations as in (
2.34
), for all
(k_{1},...,k_{p})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{p}
we have

\text{Cov}\left[{\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2},{g_{{k_{1}},...,k_{p}}}}\right]=\\
\frac{2}{n}\left\{{\left[{{\beta_{j}}E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+2}\prod\limits_{m\neq j}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}}\right)+\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+1}X_{1j^{\prime}}^{{k_{j^{\prime}}}+1}\prod\limits_{m\neq j,j^{\prime}}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}}\right)}}\right]{\beta_{j}}-\beta_{j}^{2}{E\left(\prod\limits_{m=1}^{p}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)}}\right\}

Summing the above expressions for
j=1,\ldots,p
, yields

\begin{split}\text{Cov}\left[{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{{k_{1}},....,{k_{p}}}}}\right]&=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\text{Cov}\left[{\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2},{g_{{k_{1}},....,{k_{p}}}}}\right]\\
&=\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}}E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+2}}\prod\limits_{m\neq j}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)\\
&+\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}}E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+1}}{X_{1j^{\prime}}^{{k_{j^{\prime}}}+1}}\prod\limits_{m\neq j,j^{\prime}}{{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}}\right)\\
&-\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}{E\left(\prod\limits_{m=1}^{p}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)}}\\
&\equiv\frac{2}{n}\big({L_{1}}+{L_{2}}-{L_{3}}\big),\end{split}

(2.42)

where
L_{1},L_{2}
and
L_{3}
are just a generalization of the notation given in (
2.37
).
Again, notice that
L_{1},L_{2}
and
L_{3}
are functions of
k_{1},...,k_{p}
but this is suppressed in the notation.

We now move to calculate the RHS of (
2.41
), namely
2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{\text{Cov}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}}
. Since
\psi_{jj^{\prime}}=\beta_{j}\beta_{j^{\prime}}h_{jj^{\prime}}
we have,

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}}.

(2.43)

Again, notice the relationship between
h_{jj^{\prime}}
and
g_{k_{1}...k_{p}}:
when
j=j^{\prime}
we have
{h_{jj}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\left({X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right)}=g_{0...2...0},
(i.e., the
j
-th entry is 2 and all others are 0), and for
j\neq j^{\prime}
we have
{h_{jj^{\prime}}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}=g_{0...1...1...0},
(i.e., the
j
-th and
j
’-th entries are 1 and all other entries are 0). Hence,

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)=}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}}\\
=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{0...2...0}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}+\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{0...1...1...0}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}.

(2.44)

Now, similar to (
2.39
), for all pairs of index vectors
(k_{1},...,k_{p})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{p}
, and
(k_{1}^{\prime},...,k_{p}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{p}

{\mathop{\text{Cov}}}\left({{g_{{k_{1}},...,{k_{p}}}},{g_{k_{1}^{\prime},...,k_{p}^{\prime}}}}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\left\{{E\left({\prod\limits_{j=1}^{p}{X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+{k_{j}^{\prime}}}}}\right)-E\left({\prod\limits_{j=1}^{p}{X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}}}}\right)E\left({\prod\limits_{j=1}^{p}{X_{1j}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}}}\right)}\right\}

(2.45)

This implies that

\text{Cov}\left[{g_{0...2...0},{g_{{k_{1}},...,{k_{p}}}}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+2}}\prod\limits_{m\neq j}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)-{E\left(\prod\limits_{m=1}^{p}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)}}\right]

and

\text{Cov}\left[{{g_{{0...1...1...0}}},{g_{{k_{1}},...,{k_{p}}}}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}{E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+1}}{X_{1j^{\prime}}^{{k_{j^{\prime}}}+1}}\prod\limits_{m\neq j,j^{\prime}}{{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}}\right)}.

Hence, rewrite (
2.44
) to see that

2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{\text{Cov}\left({{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}}=2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{0...2...0}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{g_{0...1...1...0}},{g_{{k_{1}}...{k_{p}}}}}\right)}\\
=\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{j}^{2}\left[{E\left(X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+2}\prod\limits_{m\neq j}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)-{E\left(\prod\limits_{m=1}^{p}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)}}\right]+\\
\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left({X_{1j}^{{k_{j}}+1}}X_{1j^{\prime}}^{{k_{j^{\prime}}}+1}\prod\limits_{m\neq j,j^{\prime}}{X_{1m}^{{k_{m}}}}\right)=\frac{2}{n}\big(L_{1}-L_{3}+L_{2}\big),

(2.46)

which is exactly the same expression as in (
2.42
). Hence, equation (
2.41
) follows which completes the proof of Theorem
2.2
.∎

Proof of Corollary
2.2
:
Write,

{\text{Var}}\left({{T_{oracle}}}\right)=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-2\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)={\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-4\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{h_{jj^{\prime}}}}\right)+4{\text{Var}}\left({\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)}.

(2.47)

Consider

\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{h_{jj^{\prime}}}}\right)}.

We have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{h_{jj^{\prime}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{h_{jj}}}\right)+}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{h_{jj^{\prime}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{0...2...0}}}\right)+}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{0...1...1...0}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}\left[\frac{{2\beta_{j}^{2}}}{n}\left({E\left({X_{1j}^{4}}\right)-1}\right)\right]+}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}\left[{\frac{4}{n}{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{\left({E\left({X_{1j}^{4}}\right)-1}\right)}\right]+}\frac{4}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}

where the second and third equality are justified by (
2.44
) and (
2.42
) respectively.
Consider now

{\text{Var}}\left({\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)
.
Write,

\small\begin{split}{\text{Var}}\left({\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)&={\text{Cov}}\left({\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{h_{jj^{\prime}}}},\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{h_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)\\
&=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}}{j_{4}}}{{\beta_{{j_{1}}}}{\beta_{{j_{2}}}}{\beta_{{j_{3}}}}{\beta_{{j_{4}}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{h_{{j_{1}}{j_{2}}}},{h_{{j_{3}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)}\\
&=\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{{\beta_{{j_{1}}}}{\beta_{{j_{2}}}}{\beta_{{j_{3}}}}{\beta_{{j_{4}}}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}},{i_{2}}}{}{\text{Cov}}\left({{X_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{1}}{j_{2}}}},{X_{{i_{2}}{j_{3}}}}{X_{{i_{2}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)}\\
&=\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{{\beta_{{j_{1}}}}{\beta_{{j_{2}}}}{\beta_{{j_{3}}}}{\beta_{{j_{4}}}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}},{i_{2}}}{}\left[{E\left({{X_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{1}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{2}}{j_{3}}}}{X_{{i_{2}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)-E\left({{X_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{1}}{j_{2}}}}}\right)E\left({{X_{{i_{2}}{j_{3}}}}{X_{{i_{2}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)}\right]}\\
&=n^{-2}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{{\beta_{{j_{1}}}}{\beta_{{j_{2}}}}{\beta_{{j_{3}}}}{\beta_{{j_{4}}}}\sum\limits_{{i=1}}^{n}\left[{E\left({{X_{i{j_{1}}}}{X_{i{j_{2}}}}{X_{i{j_{3}}}}{X_{i{j_{4}}}}}\right)-E\left({{X_{i{j_{1}}}}{X_{i{j_{2}}}}}\right)E\left({{X_{i{j_{3}}}}{X_{i{j_{4}}}}}\right)}\right]}\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}{{\beta_{{j_{1}}}}{\beta_{{j_{2}}}}{\beta_{{j_{3}}}}{\beta_{{j_{4}}}}\left[{E\left({{X_{1{j_{1}}}}{X_{1{j_{2}}}}{X_{1{j_{3}}}}{X_{1{j_{4}}}}}\right)-E\left({{X_{1{j_{1}}}}{X_{1{j_{2}}}}}\right)E\left({{X_{1{j_{3}}}}{X_{1{j_{4}}}}}\right)}\right]},\end{split}

where the fifth equality holds since the summand is 0 for all
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
.
The summation is not zero in only three cases:

1)
j_{1}=j_{4}\neq j_{2}=j_{3}

2)
j_{1}=j_{3}\neq j_{2}=j_{4}

3)
j_{1}=j_{2}=j_{3}=j_{4}.

For the first two cases the summation equals

\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}.
For the third case the summation equals to
\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]}
. Overall we have

{\text{Var}}\left({\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)=\overbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}^{case\,1}+\overbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}^{case\,2}+\overbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]}}^{case\,3}.

Rewrite (
2.47
) to get

\displaystyle\small{\text{Var}}\left({{T_{oracle}}}\right)

\displaystyle={\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-4\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{h_{jj^{\prime}}}}\right)}+4{\text{Var}}\left({\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{{\psi_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle={\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-4\left[\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{\left({E\left({X_{1j}^{4}}\right)-1}\right)}\right]+}\frac{4}{n}\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{n}\left\{{2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{4}{n}\left\{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}}\right\}.\qed

Remark 2.4
.

Calculations for Example
2.2
.

Recall that by (
2.6
) we have

{\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{4\left({n-2}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{{\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{{\left\|\beta\right\|}^{4}}}\right]+\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{{\left\|\beta\right\|}^{4}}}\right].

Now, when we assume standard Gaussian covariates, one can verify that

{\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}=\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+{\tau^{4}}

and

\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}=p{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}},
where
\sigma_{Y}^{2}=\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2}.
Thus, in this case we can write

\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{4}{n}\left[{\frac{{\left({n-2}\right)}}{{\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+{\tau^{4}}}\right]+\frac{1}{{2\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({p{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}}}\right)}\right].

(2.48)

Plug-in
\tau^{2}=\sigma^{2}=1
to get

\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})=\frac{20}{n}+O(n^{-2}),

(2.49)

and
\text{Var}(T_{oracle})=\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})-\frac{8}{n}\tau^{4}=\frac{12}{n}+O(n^{-2})
by (
2.12
).
More generally, the asymptotic improvement of
T_{oracle}
over the naive estimator is:

\displaystyle\small\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n,p\to\infty}\frac{{\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\text{Var}\left({{T_{oracle}}}\right)}}{{\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n,p\to\infty}\frac{{8{\tau^{4}}/n}}{{\frac{4}{n}\left[{\frac{{\left({n-2}\right)}}{{\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+{\tau^{4}}}\right)+\frac{1}{{2\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({p{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}}}\right)}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{2{\tau^{4}}}}{{3{\tau^{4}}+\frac{{4p{\tau^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}}}}{{2n}}}}=\frac{2}{{3+2\frac{p}{n}}},

where we used the fact that
\sigma_{Y}^{2}=\tau^{2}+\sigma^{2}=2\tau^{2}
in the second equality.
Now, notice that when
p=n
then the reduction is
\frac{2}{{3+2}}=40\%
and when
p/n
converges to zero, the reduction is
66\%.

In order to verify the above results, we repeated the simulation study from Section
2.5
but with Gaussian covariates,
considering only the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
and the OOE estimator
T_{oracle}.

For the low-dimensional case, we fixed
p=3
and considered
\beta_{j}^{2}=\frac{{\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}}}{2}
for
j=1,\dots,2
,
and
\beta_{3}^{2}={{\tau^{2}-\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}}}.

Table
4
suggests that the OOE estimator achieves similar reduction in variance as claimed in theory, namely that for the low-dimensional case, the reduction is about 66% and when
n=p
the reduction is about 40%.
