Proof of Proposition
2.3
:

Write,

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left(T\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left[{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-4{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\right)+4\text{Var}\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\right).

(2.50)

We start with calculating the middle term. Let
p_{n}(k)\equiv n(n-1)(n-2)\cdots(n-k).
Write,

\displaystyle{\text{Cov}}\big({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\big)

\displaystyle={\text{Cov}}\left({\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}}},\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)\left({n-2}\right)}}\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}\neq{i_{3}}}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j^{\prime}}}\left[{{X_{{i_{3}}j}}{X_{{i_{3}}j^{\prime}}}-E\left({{X_{{i_{3}}j}}{X_{{i_{3}}j^{\prime}}}}\right)}\right]}}}\right)

\displaystyle=C_{n}\sum\limits_{I}{\sum\limits_{J}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{1}}}},{W_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}}{W_{{i_{4}}{j_{3}}}}\left[{{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{3}}}}-E\left({{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{3}}}}}\right)}\right]}\right)}},

(2.51)

where
C_{n}\equiv\frac{1}{p_{n}(1)\cdot p_{n}(2)},

I
is the set of all quintuples of indices
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5})
such that
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
and
i_{3}\neq i_{4}\neq i_{5},
and
J
is the set of all triples of indices
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})
.
For the set
I
, there are
\binom{2}{1}\cdot 3=6
different cases to consider when one of
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
is equal to one of
\{i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\}
, and an additional
\binom{2}{2}\cdot 3!=6
cases to consider when two of
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
are equal to two of
\{i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\}
.
Similarly, for the set
J
there are three cases to consider when only two indices of
\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\}
are equal to each other, (e.g.,
j_{1}=j_{2}\neq j_{3}
) ; one case to consider when no pair of indices is equal to each other and; one case to consider when all three indices are equal.
Thus, there are total of
(6+6)\times(3+1+1)=60
cases to consider. Here we demonstrate only one such case.
Let
{I_{1}}=\left\{{\left({{i_{1}},\dots,{i_{5}}}\right):{i_{1}}={i_{5}}\neq{i_{2}}\neq{i_{3}}\,\neq\,{i_{4}}}\right\}
and
{J_{1}}=\left\{{\left({{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}}}\right):{j_{1}}={j_{2}}={j_{3}}}\right\}.

Write,

\begin{split}&C_{n}\sum\limits_{I_{1}}{\sum\limits_{J_{1}}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{1}}}},{W_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}}{W_{{i_{4}}{j_{3}}}}\left[{{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{3}}}}-E\left({{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{5}}{j_{3}}}}}\right)}\right]}\right)}}\\
&=C_{n}\sum\limits_{I_{1}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\text{Cov}\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j}}},{W_{{i_{3}}{j}}}{W_{{i_{4}}{j}}}\left[{X_{{i_{1}}{j}}^{2}-1}\right]}\right)}}\\
&=C_{n}\sum\limits_{I_{1}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E({{W_{{i_{2}}{j}}})E({W_{{i_{3}}{j}}})E({W_{{i_{4}}{j}}})}E\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j}}}\left[{X_{{i_{1}}{j}}^{2}-1}\right]}\right)}}\\
&=C_{n}\sum\limits_{I_{1}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{{j}}^{3}E\left({{W_{{i}{j}}}\left[{X_{{i}{j}}^{2}-1}\right]}\right)}.\end{split}

(2.52)

Now, notice that

\begin{split}E\left[{{W_{ij}}\left({X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right)}\right]&=E\left[{{X_{ij}}{Y_{i}}\left({X_{ij}^{2}-1}\right)}\right]\\
&=E\left[{X_{ij}^{3}\left({{\beta^{T}}X+{\varepsilon_{i}}}\right)}\right]-{\beta_{j}}\\
&={\beta_{j}}E\left({X_{ij}^{4}}\right)-{\beta_{j}}\\
&=\beta_{j}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1].\end{split}

(2.53)

Rewrite (
2.52
) to get

\displaystyle C_{n}\sum\limits_{I_{1}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{{j}}^{3}E\left[{W_{i{j}}\left({X_{i{j}}^{2}-1}\right)}\right]}}

\displaystyle=C_{n}\sum\limits_{I_{1}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{3}\left(\beta_{j}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]\right)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{n}(3)}{p_{n}(1)\cdot p_{n}(2)}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]

\displaystyle=\frac{(n-3)}{n(n-1)}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}}[E(X_{ij}^{4})-1]+O(n^{-2}),

where we used (
2.53
) to justify the first equality.
By the same type of calculation, one can compute the covariance in (
2.51
) over all 60 and obtain that

{\text{Cov}}\big({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\big)=\frac{2}{n}\left\{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}}\right\}+O\left({{n^{-2}}}\right).

(2.54)

We now move to calculate the last term of (
Supplementary Material
). Recall that

\hat{\psi}_{jj^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)\left({n-2}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}\neq{i_{3}}}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j^{\prime}}}\left[{{X_{{i_{3}}j}}{X_{{i_{3}}j^{\prime}}}-E\left({{X_{{i_{3}}j}}{X_{{i_{3}}j^{\prime}}}}\right)}\right]}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Var}\big({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\big)=\sum\limits_{J}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\psi}}_{{j_{1}}{j_{2}}}},{{\hat{\psi}}_{{j_{3}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)}

(2.55)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{{{\left[{n\left({n-1}\right)\left({n-2}\right)}\right]}^{2}}}}\sum\limits_{J}{{\text{Cov}}\left({\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}\neq{i_{3}}}{{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}}},\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}\neq{i_{3}}}{{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{3}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{4}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{3}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{4}}}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=p_{n}^{-2}(2)\sum\limits_{J}{\sum\limits_{I}{{\text{Cov}}\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}},{W_{{i_{4}}{j_{3}}}}{W_{{i_{5}}{j_{4}}}}{X_{{i_{6}}{j_{3}}}}{X_{{i_{6}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)}\,,}

where
J
is now defined to be the set of all quadruples
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}),
and
I
is now defined to be the set of all sextuples
(i_{1},...,i_{6})
such that
i_{1}\neq i_{2}\neq i_{3}
and
i_{4}\neq i_{5}\neq i_{6}.

For the set
I
, there are three different cases to consider: (1) when one of
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
is equal to one of
\{i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\}
; (2) when two of
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
are equal to two of
\{i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\};
and (3) when
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
are equal to
\{i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\}.

There are
\binom{3}{1}\cdot 3=9
options for the first case,
\binom{3}{2}\cdot 3!=18
for the second case, and
\binom{3}{3}\cdot 3!=6
options for the third case.
For the set
J
, there are five different cases to consider: (1) when there is only
one
pair of equal indices (e.g.,
j_{1}=j_{2}\neq j_{3}\neq j_{4}
);
(2) when there are
two
pairs of equal indices (e.g.,
j_{1}=j_{2}\neq j_{3}=j_{4}
);
(3) when only three indices are equal (e.g.,
j_{1}=j_{2}=j_{3}\neq j_{4}
); (4) when all four indices are equal and; (5) all four indices are different from each other. Note that there are
\binom{4}{2}=6
combinations for the first case,
\binom{4}{2}=6
for the second case,
\binom{4}{3}=4
combinations for the third case, and a single combination for each of the last two cases. Thus, there are total of
(9+18+6)\times(6+6+4+1+1)=594.
Again we demonstrate only one such calculation.
Let
{I_{2}}=\left\{{\left({{i_{1}},...,{i_{6}}}\right):{i_{1}}={i_{4}},{i_{2}}={i_{5}},{i_{3}}={i_{6}}}\right\}
and
{J_{2}}=\left\{{\left({{j_{1}},{j_{2}},{j_{3}},{j_{4}}}\right):{j_{1}}={j_{3}}\neq{j_{2}}={j_{4}}}\right\}.

In the view of (
2.55
),

\displaystyle p_{n}^{-2}(2)\sum\limits_{{J_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{I_{2}}}{\text{Cov}\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}},{W_{{i_{4}}{j_{3}}}}{W_{{i_{5}}{j_{4}}}}{X_{{i_{6}}{j_{3}}}}{X_{{i_{6}}{j_{4}}}}}\right)}}=

\displaystyle=p_{n}^{-2}(2)\sum\limits_{{J_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{I_{2}}}{\text{Cov}\left({{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}},{W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}}{W_{{i_{2}}{j_{2}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{1}}}}{X_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=p_{n}^{-2}(2)\sum\limits_{{J_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{I_{2}}}{E\left({W_{{i_{1}}{j_{1}}}^{2}}\right)E\left({W_{{i_{2}}{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)E\left({X_{{i_{3}}{j_{1}}}^{2}}\right)E\left({X_{{i_{3}}{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=p_{n}^{-2}(2)\sum\limits_{{J_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{I_{2}}}{\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{{j_{1}}}^{2}\left\{{E\left({X_{i{j_{1}}}^{4}}\right)-1}\right\}}\right)\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{{j_{2}}}^{2}\left\{{E\left({X_{i{j_{2}}}^{4}}\right)-1}\right\}}\right)}}

\displaystyle\leq p_{n}^{-2}(2)\sum\limits_{{J_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{I_{2}}}{\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{{j_{1}}}^{2}\left({C-1}\right)}\right)\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{{j_{2}}}^{2}\left({C-1}\right)}\right)}}

\displaystyle=p_{n}^{-1}(2)\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}\neq{j_{2}}}{\left[{\sigma_{Y}^{4}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}\left({C-1}\right)\left({\beta_{{j_{1}}}^{2}+\beta_{{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)+{{\left({C-1}\right)}^{2}}\beta_{{j_{1}}}^{2}\beta_{{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right]}

\displaystyle=p_{n}^{-1}(2)\left[{p\left({p-1}\right)\sigma_{Y}^{4}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}\left({C-1}\right)\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}\neq{j_{2}}}{\left({\beta_{{j_{1}}}^{2}+\beta_{{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)}+{{\left({C-1}\right)}^{2}}\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}\neq{j_{2}}}{\beta_{{j_{1}}}^{2}\beta_{{j_{2}}}^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq p_{n}^{-1}(2)\left[{p\left({p-1}\right)\sigma_{Y}^{4}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}\left({C-1}\right)\left({2p{\tau^{2}}}\right)+{{\left({C-1}\right)}^{2}}{\tau^{4}}}\right],

where the fourth equality we use
E\left({W_{ij}^{2}}\right)=\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\beta_{j}^{2}[E(X_{ij}^{2})-1]
, which is given by (
2.28
), and in the fifth equality we used the assumption that
E(X_{ij}^{4})\leq C
for some positive
C.

Since we assume
p/n=O(1),

the above expression can be further simplified to

\frac{{{p^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}}}{{{n^{3}}}}~+~O\left({{n^{-2}}}\right).

By the same type of calculation, one can
compute the covariance in (
2.55
) over all 594 cases and obtain that

\text{Var}\big({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\big)=\frac{1}{n}\left\{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}}\right\}+\frac{{{2p^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}}}{{{n^{3}}}}~+~O\left({{n^{-2}}}\right).

(2.56)

Lastly, plug-in (
2.54
) and (
2.56
) into (
Supplementary Material
) to get

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left(T\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-4{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\right)+4\text{Var}\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}{{{\hat{\psi}}_{jj^{\prime}}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-4\left(\frac{2}{n}\left\{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle+4\left({\frac{1}{n}\left\{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}}\right\}+\frac{{{2p^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}}}{{{n^{3}}}}}\right)+O\left({{n^{-2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{4}{n}\left\{{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{4}\left[{E\left({X_{1j}^{4}-1}\right)}\right]+2\sum\limits_{j\neq j^{\prime}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\beta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}}}}\right\}+\frac{{{8p^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}}}{{{n^{3}}}}+O({n^{-2}})

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{T_{oracle}}}\right)+\frac{{4{p^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}}}{{{n^{3}}}}+O\left({{n^{-2}}}\right),

where the last equality holds by (
2.12
). ∎

Remark 2.5
.

Calculations for equation
2.16
:

Write,

\displaystyle\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{n}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left({\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}}},\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{g_{i}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{E\left({{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}{g_{i}}}\right)}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{g_{{i_{1}}}}}\right)E\left({{W_{{i_{2}}j}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{{{n^{2}}\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{g_{{i_{1}}}}}\right){\beta_{j}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({{S_{ij}}}\right){\beta_{j}}},

where
S_{ij}\equiv W_{ij}g_{i}
. Also notice that

\text{Var}\left({{g_{n}}}\right)=\text{Var}\left({\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{g_{i}}}}\right)=\frac{{\text{Var}\left({{g_{i}}}\right)}}{n}.

Thus, by (
2.8
) we get

{c^{*}}=\frac{{{\text{Cov}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{g_{n}}}\right)}}{{\text{Var}\left({{g_{n}}}\right)}}=\frac{{2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{E\left({{S_{ij}}}\right){\beta_{j}}}}}{{\text{Var}\left({{g_{i}}}\right)}}.

Remark 2.6
.

Calculations for Example
2.3
:

In order to calculate
\text{Var}(T_{c^{*}})
we need to calculate the numerator and denominator of (
2.17
). Consider first
\theta_{j}\equiv E(S_{ij}).

Write,

\begin{array}[]{l}{\theta_{j}}\equiv E\left({{S_{ij}}}\right)=E\left({{X_{ij}}{Y_{i}}{g_{i}}}\right)=E\left({{X_{ij}}\left({{\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}+\varepsilon_{i}}\right){g_{i}}}\right)=\\
E\left({{X_{ij}}\left({\sum\limits_{m=1}^{p}{{\beta_{m}}{X_{im}}}+\varepsilon_{i}}\right)\sum\limits_{k<k^{\prime}}{{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}}\right)=\sum\limits_{m=1}^{p}{}\sum\limits_{k<k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{m}}E\left({{X_{ij}}{X_{im}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}\right)}\end{array}

where in the last equality we used the assumption that
E(\varepsilon|X)=0
.
Since the columns of
\bf{X}
are independent, the summation is not zero (up to permutations) when
j=k
and
m=k^{\prime}
. In this case we have

{\theta_{j}}=\sum\limits_{m=1}^{p}{\sum\limits_{k<k^{\prime}}{{\beta_{m}}E\left({{X_{ij}}{X_{im}}{X_{ik}}{X_{ik^{\prime}}}}\right)}}=\sum\limits_{m\neq j}^{p}{{\beta_{m}}E\left({X_{ij}^{2}X_{im}^{2}}\right)}=\sum\limits_{m\neq j}^{p}{{\beta_{m}}E\left({X_{ij}^{2}}\right)E\left({X_{im}^{2}}\right)}=\sum\limits_{m\neq j}^{p}{{\beta_{m}}}.

Notice that in the fourth equality we used the assumption that
E(X_{ij}^{2})=1
for all
j=1,...,p.

Thus,

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}E\left({{S_{ij}}}\right)=}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}\sum\limits_{m\neq j}^{p}{{\beta_{m}}}=}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}\left({\sum\limits_{m=1}^{p}{{\beta_{m}}}-{\beta_{j}}}\right)={{\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}}\right)}^{2}}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\beta_{j}^{2}}=}{\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}}\right)^{2}}-{\tau^{2}}.

(2.57)

plug-in
\tau^{2}=1
and
\beta_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}
to get
the numerator of (
2.17
):

{\left[{2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}E\left({{S_{ij}}}\right)}\right]^{2}}=4{\left[{{{\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}}\right)}^{2}}-{\tau^{2}}}\right]^{2}}=4{\left[{{{\left({p\frac{1}{{\sqrt{p}}}}\right)}^{2}}-1}\right]^{2}}=4{\left({{p}-1}\right)^{2}}.

Consider now the denominator of (
2.17
).
Write,

\text{Var}\left({{g_{i}}}\right)=E\left({g_{i}^{2}}\right)=E\left[{{{\left({\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}}\right)}^{2}}}\right]=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}<{j_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{j_{3}}<{j_{4}}}{E\left({{X_{i{j_{1}}}}{X_{i{j_{2}}}}{X_{i{j_{3}}}}{X_{i{j_{4}}}}}\right)}}.

Since we assume that the columns of
X
are independent, the summation is not zero when
{j_{1}}={j_{3}}
and

{j_{2}}={j_{4}}.
Thus,

\text{Var}\left({{g_{i}}}\right)=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}<{j_{2}}}{E\left({X_{i{j_{1}}}^{2}X_{i{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}<{j_{2}}}{E\left({X_{i{j_{1}}}^{2}}\right)E\left({X_{i{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)}}=p\left({p-1}\right)/2.

(2.58)

Notice that we used the assumption that since we assume that
{\bf{\Sigma}}={\bf{I}}
in the last equality.
Now, recall by (
2.49
) that

\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{20}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right).

Therefore, we have

\text{Var}\left({{T_{{c^{*}}}}}\right)=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{{{{\left[{2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}E\left({{S_{ij}}}\right)}}\right]}^{2}}}}{{n\text{Var}\left({{g_{i}}}\right)}}=\frac{{20}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right)-\frac{{4{{\left({p-1}\right)}^{2}}}}{{n\cdot\left[{p\left({p-1}\right)/2}\right]}}=\frac{{12}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right),

(2.59)

where we used the assumption that
n=p
in the last equality.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
:

We need to prove that

\sqrt{n}\left[{{T_{{c^{*}}}}-{{T}_{{\hat{c}^{*}}}}}\right]\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.

Write,

\sqrt{n}\left[{{T_{c^{*}}}-T_{\hat{c}^{*}}}\right]=\sqrt{n}\left[{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-{c^{*}}{g_{n}}-\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}-{{\hat{c}}^{*}}{g_{n}}}\right)}\right]={\sqrt{n}{g_{n}}}{\left({{{\hat{c}}^{*}}-{c^{*}}}\right)}.

By Markov and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle P\left\{{\left|{\sqrt{n}{g_{n}}\left({{\hat{c}^{*}}-{{c}^{*}}}\right)}\right|>\varepsilon}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{E\left\{{\left|{\sqrt{n}{g_{n}}\left({{\hat{c}^{*}}-{{c}^{*}}}\right)}\right|}\right\}}}{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{{\sqrt{nE\left({g_{n}^{2}}\right)E\left[{{{\left({{\hat{c}^{*}}-{{c}^{*}}}\right)}^{2}}}\right]}}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\text{Var}(g)\text{Var}(\hat{c}^{*})}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0.

(2.60)

Notice that by (
2.18
) we have

\text{Var}\left({{g}}\right)\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{c}}^{*}}}\right)=\frac{{\text{Var}\left(U\right)}}{{\text{Var}\left({{g}}\right)}},

(2.61)

where
U\equiv\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}W_{i_{2}j}g_{i_{2}}}.

The variance of
g
is

\displaystyle\text{Var}({{g}})

\displaystyle=E({g^{2}})=E[{{{({\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}})^{2}}}}]=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}<{j_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{{j_{3}}<{j_{4}}}{E\left({{X_{i{j_{1}}}}{X_{i{j_{2}}}}{X_{i{j_{3}}}}{X_{i{j_{4}}}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}<{j_{2}}}{E\left({X_{i{j_{1}}}^{2}X_{i{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)=\sum\limits_{{j_{1}}<{j_{2}}}{E\left({X_{i{j_{1}}}^{2}}\right)E\left({X_{i{j_{2}}}^{2}}\right)}}=p\left({p-1}\right)/2,

(2.62)

where the equation above holds since we assume
that
{\bf{\Sigma}}={\bf{I}}
and
that the columns of
X
are independent.
Hence, by (
Supplementary Material
) - (
Supplementary Material
) it enough to prove
\frac{\text{Var}(U)}{p^{2}}\rightarrow 0.

The variance of
U
is

\displaystyle\text{Var}(U)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left[{\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}g_{i_{2}}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{{{n^{2}}{{\left({n-1}\right)}^{2}}}}\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}^{p}{\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}},{i_{3}}\neq{i_{4}}}{\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{{i_{1}}j}}{W_{{i_{2}}j}}g_{i_{2}},{W_{{i_{3}}j^{\prime}.}}{W_{{i_{4}}j^{\prime}}}g_{i_{4}}}\right]}}

(2.63)

The covariance in (
Supplementary Material
) is different from zero in the two following cases:

1.

When
\left\{{{i_{1}},{i_{2}}}\right\}
equals to
\left\{{{i_{3}},{i_{4}}}\right\}.

2.

When one of
\left\{{{i_{1}},{i_{2}}}\right\}
equals to
\left\{{{i_{3}},{i_{4}}}\right\}
while the other is different.

The first condition includes two different sub-cases and each of those consists
n(n-1)
quadruples
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
that satisfy the condition.
Similarly, the second condition above includes four different sub-cases and each of those consists
n(n-1)(n-2)
quadruples that satisfy the condition.

We now calculate the covariance for all these six sub-cases.

(1) The covariance when
{i_{1}}={i_{3}},{i_{2}}={i_{4}}
is

\displaystyle{\delta_{1}}

\displaystyle\equiv\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{j}}{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g},{W_{j^{\prime}}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}}\right]=E\left({{W_{j}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}}\right)E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}^{2}}\right]-E\left({{W_{j}}}\right)E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g}}\right]E\left({{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}}\right)E\left[{{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left({{W_{j}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}}\right)E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}^{2}}\right]-{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}},

(2.64)

where
\tilde{W}
and
\tilde{g}
are independent copies of
W
and
g
respectively.

(2) The covariance when and
{i_{1}}={i_{4}},{i_{2}}={i_{3}}
is

\displaystyle{\delta_{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{j}}{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g},{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}g}\right]=E\left[{{W_{j}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}g}\right]E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}}\right]-E\left(W\right)E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g}}\right]E\left({{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}}\right)E\left[{{W_{j^{\prime}}}g}\right]

\displaystyle={\left\{{E\left[{{W_{j}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}g}\right]}\right\}^{2}}-{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}.

(2.65)

(3) The covariance when
\,{i_{1}}={i_{3}},{i_{2}}\neq{i_{4}}
is

{\delta_{3}}\equiv\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{j}}{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g},{W_{j^{\prime}}}{{\tilde{\tilde{W}}}_{j^{\prime}}}\tilde{\tilde{g}}}\right]=E\left({{W_{j}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}}\right){\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}-{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}},

(2.66)

where
\tilde{\tilde{W}}
and
\tilde{\tilde{g}}
are another independent copies of
W
and
g
respectively.

(4) The covariance when
i_{1}=i_{4},i_{2}\neq i_{3}
is

{\delta_{4}}\equiv\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{j}}{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g},{{\tilde{\tilde{W}}}_{j^{\prime}}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}g}\right]={\theta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left[{{W_{j}}{W_{j^{\prime}}}g}\right]-{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}.

(2.67)

(5) The covariance when
i_{2}=i_{3},i_{1}\neq i_{4}
is similar to
\delta_{4}
, i.e.,

{\delta_{5}}\equiv\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{j}}{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g},{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}{{\tilde{\tilde{W}}}_{j^{\prime}}}\tilde{\tilde{g}}}\right]={\beta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}}\right]-{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}.

(2.68)

(6) The covariance when
i_{2}=i_{4},i_{1}\neq i_{3}
is

{\delta_{6}}\equiv\text{Cov}\left[{{W_{j}}{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}\tilde{g},{{\tilde{\tilde{W}}}_{j^{\prime}}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}}\right]={\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}E\left[{{{\tilde{W}}_{j}}{{\tilde{W}_{j^{\prime}}}}\tilde{g}^{2}}\right]-{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j^{\prime}}}{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}

(2.69)

Thus, plugging-in (
Supplementary Material
) - (
2.69
) into (
Supplementary Material
) gives

\text{Var}\left(U\right)=4\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{\left\{{\frac{1}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({{\delta_{1}}+{\delta_{2}}}\right)+\frac{{\left({n-2}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({{\delta_{3}}+{\delta_{4}}+{\delta_{5}}+{\delta_{6}}}\right)}\right\}}.

(2.70)

Recall that we wish to show that
\frac{\text{Var}(U)}{p^{2}}\rightarrow 0.
Since we assume that
n/p=O(1),

it is enough to show that

\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{\frac{{\left({{\delta_{1}}+{\delta_{2}}}\right)}}{{{n^{4}}}}}\to 0

and

\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}}{\frac{{\left({{\delta_{3}}+{\delta_{4}}+{\delta_{5}}+{\delta_{6}}}\right)}}{{{n^{3}}}}}\to 0
.
Careful calculations, which are not presented here, show that under the linear model when the covariates are independent

\displaystyle\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\tau^{2}\sigma^{2}p^{2}\frac{p-1}{2},~\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{2}\leq C^{2}\tau^{3}p^{3}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and},

\displaystyle\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{3}

\displaystyle\leq p^{2}\tau^{2},\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{4}\leq C\tau^{4}p^{2},~\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{5}\leq C\tau^{2}(\tau^{2}+\sigma^{2})\frac{p(p-1)}{2},

where
C
is a bound on

E(X_{ij}^{8})
for all
j.

It follows that
\frac{\text{Var}(U)}{p^{2}}\to 0
because
n/p=O(1)
, which completes the proof of the proposition.

Remark 2.7
.

We now calculate the asymptotic improvement of
T_{\bf{B}}
over the naive estimator. For simplicity, consider the case when
\tau^{2}=\sigma^{2}=1.

Recall the variance of
\hat{\tau}^{2}
and
T_{\bf{B}}
given in (
2.6
) and (
2.21
), respectively. Write,

\displaystyle\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n,p\to\infty}\frac{{\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\text{Var}\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}}{{\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n,p\to\infty}\frac{{8{\tau_{\bf{B}}^{4}}/n}}{{\frac{4}{n}\left[{\frac{{\left({n-2}\right)}}{{\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+{\tau^{4}}}\right)+\frac{1}{{2\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({p{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}}}\right)}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{2{\tau_{\bf{B}}^{4}}}}{{3{\tau^{4}}+\frac{{4p{\tau^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}}}}{{2n}}}}=\frac{0.5}{{3+2\frac{p}{n}}},

where we used (
2.6
) in the first equality, and the fact that
\sigma_{Y}^{2}=2\tau^{2}=2
in the second equality.
Now, notice that when
p=n
and
\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}=0.5
then the reduction is
\frac{0.5}{{3+2}}=10\%
and when
p/n
converges to zero, the reduction is
16\%.

Remark 2.8
.

Calculations for Example
2.4
:

Consider the first scenario where
\beta_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{p}.

Recall that we assume that the set
B
is a fixed set of indices such that
\left|{\bf{B}}\right|\ll p
. Therefore, we have
\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}=\sum\limits_{j\in{\bf{B}}}{\beta_{j}^{2}=O\left({\frac{1}{p}}\right)}
.
Now, by (
2.22
) we have
\text{Var}(T_{\textbf{B}})=\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})-\frac{8}{n}\tau^{2}_{\textbf{B}}+O(n^{-2})
and by Remark
2.6
we have
\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})=\frac{20}{n}+O(n^{-2}).

Using the assumption that
n=p
we can conclude that
\text{Var}\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)=\frac{{20}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right).

Hence, in this scenario,
T_{\textbf{B}}
and the naive estimator have the same asymptotic variance. In contrast, recall that in Example
2.3
we showed that the asymptotic variance of
T_{c^{*}}
is
40\%
lower than the variance of the naive estimator.

Consider now the second scenario where
\hat{\tau}_{\bf{B}}^{2}={\tau^{2}}=1
. By (
2.22
) we have

\text{Var}\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)={\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{8}{n}\tau_{\bf{B}}^{4}}+O(n^{-2})=\frac{12}{n}+O(n^{-2}).

Hence, in this scenario the asymptotic variance of
T_{\textbf{B}}
is
40\%
smaller than the variance of the naive estimator.
Consider now
\text{Var}(T_{c^{*}}).
By Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

{\left({\sum\limits_{j\in{\bf{B}}}{{\beta_{j}}}}\right)^{2}}\leq\sum\limits_{j\in{\bf{B}}}{\beta_{j}^{2}\cdot\left|{\bf{B}}\right|}=\tau^{2}_{\textbf{B}}\left|{\bf{B}}\right|=O(1)
, where the last equality holds since we assume that
{\bf{B}}\subset\left\{{1,...,p}\right\}

be a fixed set of some indices such that

\left|{\bf{B}}\right|\ll p.
Now,
By (
2.57
) we have

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}{\theta_{j}}={\left({\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}}\right)^{2}}-{\tau^{2}}={\left({\sum\limits_{j\in{\bf{B}}}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}+\overbrace{\sum\limits_{j\notin{\bf{B}}}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}}}^{0}}\right)^{2}}-\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}\leq\left|{\bf{B}}\right|-1=O\left(1\right).

Now, recall that

\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{20}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right)
and
\text{Var}(g_{i})=p(p-1)/2
by (
2.49
) and (
2.58
) respectively.
Therefore, we have

\text{Var}\left({{T_{{c^{*}}}}}\right)=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{{{{\left[{2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{\beta_{j}}\theta_{j}}}\right]}^{2}}}}{{n\text{Var}\left({{g_{i}}}\right)}}=\frac{{20}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right)-O\left(\frac{1}{np^{2}}\right)=\frac{{20}}{n}+O\left({\frac{1}{{{n^{2}}}}}\right),

Hence, in this scenario,
T_{c^{*}}
and the naive estimator have the same asymptotic variance.

Lastly, recall that in Example
2.2
we already showed that, asymptotically, the variance of
T_{oracle}
(i.e., the optimal oracle estimator) is
40\%
lower than the naive variance (without any assumptions about the structure of the coefficient vector
\beta
).

Proof of Proposition
2.5
:

In order to prove that
\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0,
it is enough to show that

\displaystyle E\left\{{\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}\to 0,

(2.71)

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left\{{\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}\to 0.

(2.72)

We start with the first equation.
Let
A
denote the event that the selection algorithm
\gamma
perfectly identifies the set of large coefficients, i.e.,
A=\left\{{{{\bf{B}}_{\gamma}}={\bf{B}}}\right\}.
Let
p_{A}\equiv P(A)
denote the probability that
A
occurs, and let
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
denote the indicator of
A.
Notice that
E(T_{\textbf{B}})=\tau^{2}
and
{T_{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}={T_{\rm B}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}.
Thus,

\displaystyle E\left\{{\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}=\sqrt{n}\left[{E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)-{\tau^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\left(E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{A})}\right]+E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}}\right]-{\tau^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{A})}\right]+\sqrt{n}\left[E\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}}\right)-{\tau^{2}}\right],

(2.73)

where the last equality holds since
{T_{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}={T_{\rm B}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}.

For the convenience of notation, let
C
be an upper bound of the maximum over all first four moments of
{T_{\gamma}}
and
T_{\bf{B}},
and consider the first term of (
2.73
). By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\sqrt{n}E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}\left({1-\mathbbm{1}_{A})}\right)}\right]\leq\sqrt{n}{\left\{{E\left[{T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right]}\right\}^{1/2}}{\left\{{E\left[{{{\left({1-\mathbbm{1}_{A}}\right)}^{2}}}\right]}\right\}^{1/2}}\leq\sqrt{n}{C^{1/2}}{\left\{{1-{p_{A}}}\right\}^{1/2}}\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}0,

(2.74)

where the last inequality holds since
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}n\left({1-{p_{A}}}\right)^{1/2}=0
by assumption.
We now consider the second term of (
2.73
). Write,

\sqrt{n}\left[{E\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}}\right)-{\tau^{2}}}\right]=\sqrt{n}E\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)=-\sqrt{n}E\left[{{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-\mathbbm{1}_{A}}\right)}\right],

(2.75)

and notice that
by the same type of argument as in (
2.74
) we have

\sqrt{n}E\left[{{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-\mathbbm{1}_{A}}\right)}\right]\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}~0.

This completes the proof of (
2.71
).

We now move to show that

\text{Var}\left\{{\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}\to 0
. Write,

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left\{{\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}=n\text{Var}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=n\left[{\text{Var}\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)+\text{Var}\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)-2{\text{Cov}}\left({{T_{\gamma}},{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)-{{\left[{E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]}^{2}}+E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)-{\tau^{4}}-2\left[{E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right){\tau^{2}}}\right]}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)+E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)+E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)\left[{{\tau^{2}}-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]-{\tau^{2}}\left[{{\tau^{2}}-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{\underbrace{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}_{{\theta_{1}}}+\underbrace{E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}_{{\theta_{2}}}-\underbrace{{{\left[{{\tau^{2}}-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]}}}_{{\theta_{3}}}}^{2}\right\}.

Thus, it is enough to show that
n\theta_{1}\rightarrow 0
,
n\theta_{2}\rightarrow 0
and
n\theta_{3}\rightarrow 0.

We start with showing that
n\theta_{1}\rightarrow 0.
Notice that
T_{\textbf{B}}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{A}=T_{\textbf{B}}T_{\gamma}\mathbbm{1}_{A}=T_{\gamma}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{A}

Thus,

\displaystyle n{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left[{T_{\gamma}^{2}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]}\right\}.

Now, notice that
n(E\left[{T_{\gamma}^{2}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right])\rightarrow 0
by similar arguments as in (
2.74
), with a slight modification of using the existence of the fourth moments of
T_{\gamma}
and
T_{\bf{B}}
, rather than the second moments. Also, by Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we have,

\displaystyle nE\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{1_{A}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq n{\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{2}T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)}\right\}^{1/2}}{\left\{{E\left[{{{\left({1-{1_{A}}}\right)}^{2}}}\right]}\right\}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq n{\left\{{E\left({T_{\gamma}^{4}}\right)E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{4}}\right)}\right\}^{1/4}}{\left\{{1-{p_{A}}}\right\}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq n{C^{1/2}}{\left\{{1-{p_{A}}}\right\}^{1/2}}\to 0,

where
C
is an upper bound of the maximum over all first four moments of
{T_{\gamma}}
and
T_{\bf{B}}.

Therefore,
n\theta_{1}\rightarrow 0.

Consider now
n\theta_{2}.
Write,

\displaystyle n{\theta_{2}}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}}\right)-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left({T_{\bf{B}}^{2}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left[{T_{\bf{B}}^{2}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]}\right\}\rightarrow 0,

and notice that the last equation follows by similar arguments.

Consider now
n\theta_{3}.
Write,

\displaystyle n\theta_{3}

\displaystyle=n\left[{\tau^{2}-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=n\left[{E\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=n\left[{E\left[{{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)+{T_{\bf{B}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right]-E\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left[{{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]+E\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}-{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left[{{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]+E\left({{T_{\gamma}}{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}-{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=n\left\{{E\left[{{T_{\bf{B}}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]-E\left[{{T_{\gamma}}\left({1-{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}}\right)}\right]}\right\}\rightarrow 0,

where the last equation follows by similar arguments as in (
2.74
). This completes the proof of (
2.72
) and we conclude that
\sqrt{n}\left({{T_{\gamma}}-{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.6
:

We wish to prove that

\displaystyle n\left[{\widehat{\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)}-\text{Var}\left({{\hat{\tau}^{2}}}\right)}\right]\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.

(2.76)

Recall by (
2.6
) that

{\text{Var}}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{4\left({n-2}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{{\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{{\left\|\beta\right\|}^{4}}}\right]+\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{{\left\|\beta\right\|}^{4}}}\right].

Now, when we assume standard Gaussian covariates, one can verify that

{\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}=\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+{\tau^{4}}

and

\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}=p{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}},
where
\sigma_{Y}^{2}=\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2}.
Thus, in this case we can write

\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{4}{n}\left[{\frac{{\left({n-2}\right)}}{{\left({n-1}\right)}}\left[{\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+{\tau^{4}}}\right]+\frac{1}{{2\left({n-1}\right)}}\left({p{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}+4\sigma_{Y}^{2}{\tau^{2}}+3{\tau^{4}}}\right)}\right].

(2.77)

In order to prove that (
2.76
) holds, it is enough to prove the consistency of
\hat{\tau}^{2}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{Y}^{2}.

Consistency of the sample variance
\hat{\sigma}_{Y}^{2}
is a standard result, and since
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is an unbiased estimator, it is enough to show that its variance converges to zero as
n\to\infty.

Since we assume
\hat{\tau}^{2}+\sigma^{2}=O(1)
and
p/n=O(1),
we have by (
2.48
) that
\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}0
,
and (
2.76
) follows.

Proof of Proposition
2.7
:

We now move to prove that

n\left[{\widehat{\text{Var}\left({T_{\gamma}}\right)}-\text{Var}\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0,

(2.78)

Recall that by Proposition
2.5
we have

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}n\left[{\text{Var}\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)-{\text{Var}}\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}\right]=0.

Hence, it is enough to show that

n\left[{\widehat{\text{Var}\left({T_{\gamma}}\right)}-\text{Var}\left(T_{\bf{B}}\right)}\right]\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0.

Since we assume
{X_{i}}\mathop{\sim}\limits^{i.i.d}N\left({\bf{0},\bf{I}}\right)
then by (
2.21
) we have

\text{Var}\left({{T_{\bf{B}}}}\right)={\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{8}{n}\tau_{\bf{B}}^{4}}+O(n^{-2}).

Recall that by definition we have
\widehat{\text{Var}\left({{T_{\gamma}}}\right)}={\widehat{\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)}-\frac{8}{n}\hat{\tau}_{{{\bf{B}}_{\gamma}}}^{4}}.
Also recall that
\widehat{\text{Var}({\hat{\tau}}^{2}})
is consistent by Proposition
2.6
. Thus, it is enough to prove that

\hat{\tau}_{{{\bf{B}}_{\gamma}}}^{2}-\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.

Now, since we assumed that
n\left[{P\left(\left\{{{{\bf{B}}_{\gamma}}\neq{\bf{B}}}\right\}\right)}\right]^{1/2}\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{}0
then clearly
P\left({{{\bf{B}}_{\gamma}}={\bf{B}}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{}1.
Thus,
it is enough to show that
\hat{\tau}_{{{\bf{B}}}}^{2}-\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.

Recall that
E(\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2})=\beta_{j}^{2}
for
j=1,...,p
and notice that
\text{Var}(\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2})\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}0
by similar arguments that were used to derive (
2.6
). Hence, we have
\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2}-\beta_{j}^{2}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0.
Since
we assumed that
{\bf{B}}
is finite, we have

\hat{\tau}_{\bf{B}}^{2}-\tau_{\bf{B}}^{2}=\sum\limits_{j\in{\bf{B}}}{\left({\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2}-\beta_{j}^{2}}\right)}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}0,

and (
2.78
) follows.

Remark 2.9
.

We use the the following simple selection algorithm
\gamma
:

Input:

A dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{{\bf{Y}}_{n\times 1}}}\right)
.

1.

Calculate
\hat{\beta}_{1}^{2},...,\hat{\beta}_{p}^{2}
where
\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2}
is given in (
2.4
) for
j=1,...,p.

2.

Calculate the differences

{\lambda_{j}}=\hat{\beta}_{\left(j\right)}^{2}-\hat{\beta}_{\left({j-1}\right)}^{2}
for
j=2,\ldots,p
where
\hat{\beta}_{\left(1\right)}^{2}<\hat{\beta}_{\left(2\right)}^{2}<...<\hat{\beta}_{\left(p\right)}^{2}
denotes the order statistics.

3.

Select the covariates
{{\bf{B}}_{\gamma}}=\left\{{j:\hat{\beta}_{\left(j\right)}^{2}>\hat{\beta}_{\left({{j^{*}}}\right)}^{2}}\right\}
, where
{j^{*}}=\mathop{\arg\max}\limits_{j}{\lambda_{j}}
.

Result:

Return
\bf{B_{\gamma}}
.

Algorithm 3

Covariate selection
\gamma

The algorithm above finds the largest gap between the ordered estimated squared coefficients and then uses this gap as a threshold to select a set of coefficients
\bf{B_{\gamma}}\subset\left\{{1,...,p}\right\}.
The algorithm works well in scenarios where a relatively large gap truly separates between larger coefficients and the smaller coefficients of the vector
\beta
.

Remark 2.10
.

The following algorithm is used to construct the Selection estimator that improves an initial estimator of
\tau^{2}
, as presented in Table
3
.

Input:

A dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
, an estimation procedure
\tilde{\tau}^{2},
and a covariate-selection procedure
\delta
.

1.

Apply the procedure
\delta
to the dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
to obtain
{{\bf{B}}_{\delta}}.

2.

Apply the procedure
\tilde{\tau}^{2}
to the dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
.

3.

Calculate the zero-estimator

{Z_{h}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{h\left({{X_{i}}}\right)}
,
where
h\left({{X_{i}}}\right)=\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}\in{{\bf{B}}_{\delta}}}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}.

4.

Bootstrap step:

•

Sample
n
observations at random from
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
, with replacement, to obtain a bootstrap dataset.

•

Repeat steps 2 and 3 based on the bootstrap dataset.

The bootstrap step is repeated
M
times in order to produce

(\tilde{\tau}^{2})^{*1},...,(\tilde{\tau}^{2})^{*M}
and

Z_{h}^{*1},...,Z_{h}^{*M}.

5.

Approximate the coefficient
\tilde{c}_{h}^{*}=\frac{{\widehat{\text{Cov}\left({\tilde{\tau}^{2},{Z_{h}}}\right)}}}{{\text{Var}\left({{Z_{h}}}\right)}}

where
\widehat{{\text{Cov}}\left(\cdot\right)}
denotes the empirical covariance from the bootstrap samples.

Output
:
Return the empirical estimator

T_{\tilde{h}}\equiv\tilde{\tau}^{2}-\tilde{c}_{h}^{*}Z_{h}.

Algorithm 4

Empirical Estimator

The paper “A zero-estimator approach for estimating the signal level in a high-dimensional model-free setting” is
submitted to the Journal of Statistical Planning and Inference.

3
A zero-estimator approach for estimating the signal level in a high-dimensional model-free setting

3.1
Introduction

In many regression settings, an important goal is to estimate the signal and noise levels, i.e., to quantify the amount of variance in the response variable that can be explained by a set of covariates, versus how much of the variation is left unexplained.
When the covariates’ dimension is low and a linear regression model is assumed, the ordinary least squares method can be used to find a consistent estimator for the signal level.
However, in a high-dimensional setting, the least squares method breaks down and it becomes more challenging to develop good estimators without further assumptions.
In recent years, several methods have been proposed for estimating the signal level under the assumption that the regression coefficient vector
\beta
is sparse

(
Fan et al. 2012
,
Sun and Zhang 2012
,
Chatterjee and
Jafarov 2015
,
Verzelen et al. 2018
,
Tony Cai and Guo 2020
)
.
Other widely-used methods
assume some probabilistic structure on
\beta
(e.g.,
\beta
is Gaussian) and use maximum likelihood to derive consistent estimators of the signal level

(
Yang et al. 2010
,
Bonnet et al. 2015
)
.
These methods have been extensively studied in the literature of
random-effect models
where
\beta
is treated as random; see
de Los Campos et al. 2015
and references therein.
However, methods that rely on the assumption that
\beta
is either sparse or highly structured may not perform well when these assumptions fail to hold.
For example, a known problem in genetics is the problem of missing heritability
(
de Los Campos et al. 2015
,
Zhu and Zhou 2020
)
.
Heritability is defined as the fraction of the observed outcome (phenotype) that is explained by genetic factors.
The term “missing heritability” is traditionally used to describe the gap between heritability estimates from genome-wide-association-studies (GWAS) and the corresponding estimates from family studies. To explain the gap, it has been suggested that some phenotypes are explained by a numerous number of genetic factors that their individual effect is too small to detect, but their collective effect is significant
(
Yang et al. 2010
,
Young 2022
)
. In such a setting, methods that rely on the sparsity assumption may fail to provide accurate estimates.

Rather than assuming sparsity or other structural assumptions on
\beta
, a different approach for estimating the signal level in a high-dimensional setting is to assume some or complete knowledge about the covariate distribution. This can be justified, for example, in the semi-supervised setting when one has access to a large amount of unlabeled (covariate) data without the corresponding labels (responses).
When the covariates are assumed independent Gaussian,
Dicker 2014
proposed estimators
based on the method-of-moments and
Janson et al. 2017
used convex optimization techniques.
In both methods, the Gaussian assumption was used to show consistency and asymptotic-normality, and it is not clear how robust these methods are when the assumptions are violated.
Dropping the Gaussian independent covariate assumption,

Livne et al. 2021

proposed a consistent estimator
under the assumption that the first two moments of covariates are known.
More recently,
Chen 2022

proposed an estimator that
is consistent and asymptotically-normal when the covariates are independent and the entries of
\beta
are small and dense.

All of the estimators that we reviewed above were developed under the assumption that the linear model is true, which can be unrealistic in many situations.
In this work, we focus our attention on the
model-free
setting, i.e., no assumptions are made about the relationship between the covariates and the response.
Under this setting,
Kong and Valiant 2018

proposed a consistent
estimator under some assumptions on the covariance matrix.
In this paper we follow the two-stage approach presented in
Livne et al. 2021
, where an initial estimator is first suggested and then a zero-estimator is used to reduce its variance.
Our initial estimator is the same as in
Kong and Valiant 2018
and

Livne et al. 2021
, and the zero estimators we use are tailored to the model-free framework. Furthermore, we provide a general algorithm that, in principle, can improve any initial estimator and we also demonstrate the usefulness of the algorithm for several initial estimators.

The rest of this work is organized as follows.
In Section
3.2
,
we discuss the parameters of interest in a model-free setting under the assumption that the first two moments of the covariates are known.
In Section
3.3
, we present our initial estimators and prove that they are consistent under some minimal assumptions.
In Section
3.4
, we use the zero-estimator approach to construct two improved estimators and then study some theoretical properties of the improved estimators. Simulation results are given in Section
2.5
. Section
3.6
demonstrates how the zero-estimator approach can be generalized to other estimators. A discussion is given in Section
2.7
. The proofs are provided in the Appendix.

3.2
Preliminaries

Let
X\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}
be a random vector of covariates and let
Y\in\mathbb{R}
be the response.
The conditional mean
E(Y|X)
is the
best predictor
in the sense that it minimizes
the mean squared error
E\{[Y-g(X)]^{2}\}
over all measurable functions
g(X)
(see
Hansen 2022
, p.
25
).
However, the functional form of
E(Y|X)
is typically unknown and difficult to estimate, especially in a high-dimensional setting. Consequently, we can define the best linear approximation to
E(Y|X)
.

Definition 3.1
.

Assume that both
E(Y^{2})
,
E({{{\left\|X\right\|}^{2}}})
exist and that the covariance matrix of
X
, denoted by
{\Sigma_{X}}
,
is invertible. Then, the
best linear predictor
,
\alpha+\beta^{T}X
, is defined by the unique
\alpha
and
\beta
that minimize the mean squared error

(\alpha,\beta)=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{a\in\mathbb{R},b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}E(Y-a-b^{T}X)^{2},

and, by
Hansen 2022
, pp. 34-36, is given by

\beta={\Sigma_{X}}^{-1}\{E(XY)-E(X)E(Y)\}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\alpha=E(Y)-\beta^{T}E(X).

(3.1)

The best linear predictor is essentially the population version of the OLS method. Notice that
\alpha,\beta
also satisfy
(\alpha,\beta)=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{a\in\mathbb{R},b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}E[E(Y|X)-a-b^{T}X]^{2}
.
It is a model-free quantity, i.e., no specific assumptions are made about the relationship between
X
and
Y
. In particular, we do not assume that
E(Y|X)
is linear in
X
.
If
E(Y|X)
happens to be linear, say
E(Y|X)=\tilde{\alpha}+\tilde{\beta}^{T}X
, then the best linear predictor parameters
(\alpha,\beta)
coincide with the model parameters
(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}).

However, when
E(Y|X)
is not linear, the parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
are still meaningful: they describe the overall direction of the association between
X
and
Y

(
Buja et al. 2019
)
.
Hence, Definition
3.1
is useful
since in most cases we have no reason to believe that
E(Y|X)
is indeed linear in
X
.

We now wish to decompose the variance of
Y
into signal and noise levels. Let
\sigma_{Y}^{2}
denote the variance of
Y
and define the residual
\varepsilon\equiv Y-\alpha-\beta^{T}X
. Notice that both
E(X\varepsilon)=0
and
E(\varepsilon)=0
by construction. Write

\sigma_{Y}^{2}=\text{Var}(\alpha+X^{T}\beta+\varepsilon)=\beta^{T}\text{Var}(X)\beta+\text{Var}(\varepsilon)=\beta^{T}{\Sigma}_{X}\beta+\sigma^{2},

(3.2)

where
\sigma^{2}\equiv\text{Var}(\varepsilon)
and
{\Sigma}_{X}\equiv\text{Var}(X)
.
Here, the
signal
level
\tau^{2}\equiv\beta^{T}{\Sigma}_{X}\beta
can be thought of as the total variance explained by the best linear function of the covariates. The
noise
level
\sigma^{2}
is the variance left unexplained.
Notice that the parameters
\tau^{2}
,
\sigma^{2}
and
\sigma_{Y}^{2}
depend on
p
but this is suppressed in the notation.

A common starting point for many works of regression problems is to use strong assumptions about
E(Y|X)
, and minimal assumptions, if any, about the covariate
X
. In this work, we take the opposite approach: we make no assumptions about
E(Y|X)
but assume instead that the distribution of
X
is known.
This can be justified, for example, in a semi-supervised setting where, in addition to the labeled data, we also have access to a large amount of unlabeled data; see, for example, the work of
Zhang and Bradic 2019
who study estimation of the mean and variance of
Y
in a semi-supervised, model-free setting.
A sensitivity analysis of the known-covariate distribution assumption is carried out in a simulation study in Section
2.5
, where the distribution of
X
is estimated based on an unlabeled dataset.
Note that the assumption of known-covariate distribution has already been presented and discussed in the context of high-dimensional regression as in
Candes et al. 2018
,
Berrett et al. 2020
and
Wang and Janson 2020
.
Our goal here is to develop good estimators for
\tau^{2}
and
\sigma^{2}
in a model-free setting under the assumption that the distribution of the covariates is known.

Let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),...,(X_{n},Y_{n})
be i.i.d. observations drawn from an unknown distribution where
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
Y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
(X,Y)
denote a generic observation from the sample.
We assume that
E(X)\equiv\mu
is known and also that the variance matrix
{\Sigma}_{X}
is known and invertible. Linear transformations do not affect the signal and noise levels. Thus, we can apply the transformation

X\mapsto{\Sigma}_{X}^{-1/2}(X-~\mu)
and assume w.l.o.g. that

E(X)=0\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt{\Sigma}_{X}=\textbf{I}.

(3.3)

By (
3.2
),

\sigma_{Y}^{2}=\|\beta\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}
,
which means that in order to estimate
\sigma^{2}
it is enough to estimate both
\sigma_{Y}^{2}
and
\tau^{2}\equiv\|\beta\|^{2}
. The variance term
\sigma_{Y}^{2}
can be easily estimated from the sample. Hence, the main challenge is to derive an estimator for
\tau^{2}.

3.3
Initial Estimators

In this section, we present our initial estimators for the signal and noise levels,
\tau^{2}
and
\sigma^{2}
.
Interestingly, when the linear model is true and
X
is assumed to be constant, no consistent estimator of
\sigma^{2}
exists
(
Azriel 2019
)
.
However, when

X
is random, a consistent estimator does exist if
{\Sigma}
is known
(
Verzelen et al. 2018
)
.
The current work goes one step further as we generalize this result without assuming linearity.
Indeed, Proposition
3.2
below
demonstrates that by knowing the first and second moments of
X
, it is possible, under some mild assumptions, to construct consistent estimators of
\tau^{2}
and
\sigma^{2}
in a high-dimensional setting without assuming that
E(Y|X)
is linear. The estimator we use below was suggested by
Kong and Valiant 2018
who provided an upper bound on the variance. Our analysis is more general and we discuss sufficient conditions for consistency.

Let
W_{ij}\equiv X_{ij}Y_{i}
for
i=1,...,n
, and
j=1,...,p
. Notice that

E\left({{W_{ij}}}\right)=E\left({{X_{ij}}{Y_{i}}}\right)=E\left[{{X_{ij}}\left({{\alpha+\beta^{T}}{X_{i}}+{\varepsilon_{i}}}\right)}\right]={\beta_{j}},

(3.4)

where in the last equality we used (
3.3
) and the orthogonality between
X
and
\varepsilon.

Now, since

\{E(W_{ij})\}^{2}=E(W_{ij}^{2})-\text{Var}(W_{ij}),

a natural unbiased estimator for
\beta_{j}^{2}
is

{\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}W_{ij}^{2}-\frac{1}{n-1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(W_{ij}-\overline{W}_{j})^{2}=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}}^{n}W_{i_{1}j}W_{i_{2}j},

(3.5)

where
\overline{W}_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{ij}
. Thus, an unbiased estimator of
\tau^{2}
is given by

{\hat{\tau}^{2}}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2}=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}{W_{{i_{1}}}^{T}{W_{{i_{2}}}}}},

(3.6)

where
{W_{i}}=\left({{W_{i1}},...,{W_{ip}}}\right)^{T}
.
We call
\hat{\tau}^{2}
the
naive estimator
.
Notice that
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is a U-statistic with the kernel

h\left({{W_{1}},{W_{2}}}\right)=W_{1}^{T}{W_{2}}
and thus its variance can be calculated directly by using U-statistic properties (see
van der Vaart 2000
, Theorem 12.3).

Let

{\zeta_{1}}\equiv{\beta^{T}}{\bf{A}}\beta-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}
,
{\zeta_{2}}\equiv\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}-{\left\|\beta\right\|^{4}}
,

{\bf{A}}\equiv E\left({{W}W^{T}}\right)

and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm of
\bf{A}.
The following proposition calculates the variance of
\hat{\tau}^{2}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Assuming that
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
are finite,
the variance of the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is given by

\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)=\frac{{4\left({n-2}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}{\zeta_{1}}+\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}{\zeta_{2}}.

(3.7)

The next proposition shows that the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is consistent under some assumptions.

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume that
\frac{\|\textbf{A}\|^{2}_{F}}{n^{2}}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0,
and that
\tau^{2}
is bounded. Then,

\hat{\tau}^{2}-\tau^{2}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0.

Similarly, an estimator for the noise level
\sigma^{2}
can be obtained by

{\hat{\sigma}^{2}}\equiv\hat{\sigma}_{Y}^{2}-{\hat{\tau}^{2}},

(3.8)

where

\hat{\sigma}_{Y}^{2}\equiv\frac{1}{{n-1}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{{\left({{Y_{i}}-\bar{Y}}\right)}^{2}}}
is the standard unbiased estimator of
\sigma_{Y}^{2}.

Let
{\mu_{4}}\equiv E\left[{{{\left({Y-\alpha}\right)}^{4}}}\right]
,
\pi={\left({{\pi_{1}},...,{\pi_{p}}}\right)^{T}}
where
{\pi_{j}}\equiv E\left[{{{\left({Y_{1}-\alpha}\right)}^{2}}{W_{1j}}}\right]
.
The variance of
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
is given by the following proposition.

Proposition 3.3
.

The variance of
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
is

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\sigma}}^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[{\frac{1}{n}{\mu_{4}}-\frac{{\left({n-3}\right)}}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sigma_{Y}^{4}}\right]+\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{n}\left({{\pi^{T}}\beta-{\tau^{2}}\sigma_{Y}^{2}}\right)+\frac{4}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{p}{{{\left\{{E\left[{{W_{1j}}\left({Y_{1}-\alpha}\right)}\right]}\right\}}^{2}}},

(3.9)

where
\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})
is given in (
2.6
).

The following result is a corollary of Propositions 2 and 3.

Corollary 3.1
.

Assume that
\mu_{4}
and
\tau^{2}
are bounded and that
\frac{\|{\bf A}\|_{F}^{2}}{n^{2}}\rightarrow 0.
Then,

\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0.

The condition
\frac{\|{\bf A}\|_{F}^{2}}{n^{2}}\rightarrow 0
holds in various settings. For example, it can be shown to hold when
p/n^{2}\rightarrow 0
and
Y
is bounded. For more examples and details, see Remark
3.1
in the Appendix.

3.4
Reducing Variance Using a Zero-Estimator

In this section, we study how the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
, and consequently
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
, can be improved by
using the
assumption that the distribution of
X
is known.
We use zero-estimators to construct an improved unbiased estimator of
\tau^{2}
.
This is also known as the method of
control variables
from the Monte-Carlo literature; see, e.g.,
Glynn and Szechtman 2002
, and
Lavenberg and Welch 1981
.
Here, a zero-estimator is defined as a statistic
Z
such that
E(Z)=0
. For a given zero-estimator
Z
and a constant
c
, we define a new estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c)
as

\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c)=\hat{\tau}^{2}-cZ.

(3.10)

For a fixed
c
, notice that
\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c)
is an unbiased estimator for
\tau^{2}.

Also notice that for every function
f
of the covariates
X_{1},...,X_{n}
, one can always define a zero-estimator

{Z}={f\left({{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}}\right)-E\left[{f\left({{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}}\right)}\right]}.

This is possible since we assume that the distribution of the covariates is known and hence
E[f\left({{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}}\right)]
is known.
The variance of
\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c)
is

\text{Var}[\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c)]=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)+{c^{2}}\text{Var}\left({{Z}}\right)-2c\,\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{Z}}\right).

Minimizing the variance with respect to
c
yields the minimizer

{c^{*}}=\frac{{\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{Z}}\right)}}{{\text{Var}\left({{Z}}\right)}}.

(3.11)

Hence, the corresponding oracle-estimator is

\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c^{*})=\hat{\tau}^{2}-c^{*}Z.

We use the term
oracle
since the optimal coefficient
c^{*}
is an unknown quantity.
The variance of the above oracle-estimator is

\displaystyle\text{Var}[\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z,c^{*})]

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right)-\frac{{\left[{\text{Cov}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{Z}}\right)}\right]^{2}}}{{\text{Var}\left({{Z}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\left({1-\rho_{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{Z}}^{2}}\right)\text{Var}\left({{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}}}\right),

(3.12)

where
\rho_{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{Z}}
is the correlation coefficient between
\hat{\tau}^{2}
and
Z
.
The term
{1-\rho_{{{\hat{\tau}}^{2}},{Z}}^{2}}
is the factor by which
\text{Var}(\hat{\tau}^{2})
could be reduced if the optimal coefficient
c^{*}
was known.
Thus, the more correlation there is between the zero-estimator
Z
and the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
, the greater the reduction in variance.

There are two challenges to be addressed with the above approach. First, one should find a simple zero-estimator
Z
which is correlated with the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
. Second, the optimal coefficient
c^{*}
is an unknown quantity and therefore needs to be estimated.

To address the first challenge, we propose the following zero-estimator

Z_{g}\equiv{\bar{g}_{n}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{g}(X_{i})},

where

{g}(X_{i})=\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}.

In Remark
3.2
in the Appendix, we show that the optimal coefficient, with respect to
Z_{g}
, is

{c_{g}^{*}}=\frac{{{2\beta^{T}}\theta_{g}}}{{\text{Var}[{{g(X)}}]}},

(3.13)

where
{\theta_{g}}=E\left[{Wg\left(X\right)}\right].

Notice that
\text{Var}[{g(X)}]
is a known quantity since the distribution of
g(X)
is assumed to be known.
Hence, the corresponding oracle-estimator is

\displaystyle T_{g}\equiv\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z_{g},c_{g}^{*})=\hat{\tau}^{2}-c_{g}^{*}Z_{g}.

(3.14)

To address the second challenge, i.e., to estimate the optimal coefficient
c_{g}^{*}
, we suggest the following unbiased U-statistic estimator

\hat{c}_{g}^{*}=\frac{\binom{n}{2}^{-1}{\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{W_{{i_{1}}}^{T}{W_{{i_{2}}}}g\left({{X_{{i_{2}}}}}\right)}}}{{\text{Var}\left[{g\left(X\right)}\right]}}.

(3.15)

Thus, the corresponding improved estimator is

T_{\hat{g}}\equiv\hat{\tau}^{2}(Z_{g},\hat{c}_{g}^{*})={\hat{\tau}^{2}}-{\hat{c}_{g}^{*}}Z_{g}.

(3.16)

Using the zero-estimator
Z_{g}
has a potential drawback. It uses
all
the
p
covariates of the vector
X
regardless of the sparsity level in the data, which can result in some additional variability due to unnecessary estimation. Intuitively, when the sparsity level is high, i.e., only a small number of covariates plays an important role in explaining the response
Y
,
it is inefficient to use a zero-estimator that incorporates all the
p
covariates.
In such a setting, it is reasonable to modify the zero-estimator
Z_{g}
such that only
a small set of covariates will be included, preferably the covariates that capture a significant part of the signal level
\tau^{2}.

Selecting such a set of covariates can be difficult and one may use a covariate-selection procedure for this purpose.

We call
\delta
a covariate
selection procedure
if for every dataset it chooses a subset of indices
{{\bf{S}}_{\delta}}
from
\left\{{1,...,p}\right\}
.
Different covariate-selection methods exist in the literature (see
Oda et al. 2020
and references therein) but these are not a primary focus of this work.
For a given selection procedure
\delta
we modify the estimator
T_{\hat{g}}
such that only the indices in
{\bf{S}}_{\delta}
will be included in its zero-estimator term.
This modified estimator, which is based on a given selection procedure
\delta
, is presented in the algorithm below.

Input:

A dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
and a selection procedure
\delta
.

1.

Calculate the naive estimator

{\hat{\tau}^{2}}=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}<{i_{2}}}{W_{{i_{1}}}^{T}{W_{{i_{2}}}}},

2.

Apply procedure
\delta
to
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
to construct the set
{{\bf{S}}_{\delta}}.

Output
:
Return the estimator

T_{\hat{h}}\equiv{\hat{\tau}^{2}}-\hat{c}_{h}^{*}{Z_{h}},

(3.17)

where

\hat{c}_{h}^{*}=\frac{{\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{W_{{i_{1}}}^{T}{W_{{i_{2}}}}h\left({{X_{{i_{2}}}}}\right)}}}{{\text{Var}\left[{h\left({{X}}\right)}\right]}}
,

{Z_{h}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{{h}\left({{X_{i}}}\right)}
and
h\left({{X_{i}}}\right)=\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}\in{\bf{S}}_{\delta}}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}
.

Algorithm 5

Proposed estimator for
\tau^{2}.

Notice that the estimator
T_{\hat{g}}

defined in (
3.16
) is a special case of the estimator
T_{\hat{h}}

defined in Algorithm
5
, when
{\bf{S}}_{\delta}=\{1,\dots p\}
, i.e., when
\delta
selects all the
p
covariates.

Recall that in this work we treat
p
as a function of
n
, i.e.,
p\equiv p_{n}
but this is suppressed
in the notation.
Let
\Theta\equiv\Theta_{n}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,p\}
be a deterministic sequence of subsets.
In order to analyze the estimator

T_{\hat{h}}

we define a stability property, which is given next.

Definition 3.2
.

A selection procedure
\delta
is
stable

if there exists a deterministic sequence of subsets
\Theta
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}n[P({\bf S}_{\delta}\neq\Theta)]^{1/2}=0.

(3.18)

Definition
3.2
states that a selection procedure
\delta
is stable if it is asymptotically close to a deterministic procedure at a suitable rate.
The convergence rate of many practical selection procedures
is exponential,
which is much faster than is required for the condition to hold. For example, the lasso algorithm asymptotically selects the support of
\beta
at an exponential rate under some assumptions
(see
Hastie et al. 2015
, Theorem 11.3). Notice also that the stability condition holds trivially when
{\bf{S}}_{\delta}=\{1,\dots p\}
, i.e., when
\delta
selects all the
p
covariates for all
n
.

Define the oracle-estimator
{T_{h}}\equiv{\hat{\tau}^{2}}\left({{Z_{h}},c_{h}^{*}}\right)
, where
c_{h}^{*}=\frac{{{\beta^{T}}{\theta_{h}}}}{{\text{Var}\left[{h\left(X\right)}\right]}}
and
{\theta_{h}}=E\left[{Wh\left(X\right)}\right]
.
Let

\displaystyle f\left({{X_{i}}}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}\in\Theta}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}},\hskip 11.74988pt{T_{f}}\equiv{\hat{\tau}^{2}}\left({{Z_{f}},c_{f}^{*}}\right),\hskip 11.74988pt{T_{\hat{f}}}\equiv{\hat{\tau}^{2}}\left({{Z_{f}},\hat{c}_{f}^{*}}\right),\hskip 11.74988ptc_{f}^{*}\equiv\frac{{2{\beta^{T}}{\theta_{f}}}}{{\text{Var}\left[{f\left(X\right)}\right]}},

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{f}^{*}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{{\frac{2}{{n\left({n-1}\right)}}\sum\limits_{{i_{1}}\neq{i_{2}}}{W_{{i_{1}}}^{T}{W_{{i_{2}}}}f\left({{X_{{i_{2}}}}}\right)}}}{{\text{Var}\left[{f\left(X\right)}\right]}},\hskip 11.74988ptb\equiv Wf(X),\hskip 11.74988pt\theta_{b}\equiv E(b),\hskip 11.74988pt{Z_{f}}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{f\left({{X_{i}}}\right)},

\displaystyle{\bf{B}}

\displaystyle=E\left[{W{W^{T}}f\left(X\right)}\right],\hskip 11.74988pt{\bf{C}}=E\left[{W{W^{T}}f^{2}{{\left(X\right)}}}\right].

(3.19)

We now prove that the proposed estimator in Algorithm
5
is asymptotically equivalent to its oracle version
T_{h}
under some conditions.

Proposition 3.4
.

Assume that the selection procedure
\delta
is stable with respect to
\Theta
. Assume also that
n/p
,
\|\beta\|^{2}
,
\frac{{{{\left\|\theta_{b}\right\|}^{2}}}}{{\text{Var}[f(X)]}}

and

\frac{{E\left({{{\left\|b\right\|}^{2}}}\right)}}{{n\text{Var}[f(X)]}}
are bounded, and

\frac{{\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{n^{2}}}}\to 0
,
\frac{{\left\|{\bf{B}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{n^{2}\text{Var}[f(X)]}}}\to~0
,
and
\frac{{\left\|{\bf{C}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{\{n\text{Var}[f(X)]\}^{2}}}\to 0.

In addition, assume that the first four moments of
T_{h}
,
T_{\hat{h}}
,
T_{f}
, and
T_{\hat{f}}
are bounded.
Then,

\sqrt{n}\left[T_{\hat{h}}-T_{h}\right]\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0.

(3.20)

Our proof of Proposition
3.4
shows a slightly stronger result: the proposed estimator
T_{\hat{h}}
is also asymptotically equivalent to
T_{f}
, the oracle-estimator that originally knows the set of indices
\Theta
.

We now discuss the assumptions of Proposition
3.4
.
In Remark
3.1
, several sufficient conditions implying that
\frac{{\left\|{\bf{A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{n^{2}}}}\to 0
were presented . Similarly, in Remark
3.3
we show that if the covariates
X_{ij}
, for
j=1,...,p
,
i=1,...,n,
and the response
Y
are bounded, then so is

\frac{{E\left({{{\left\|b\right\|}^{2}}}\right)}}{{n\text{Var}[f(X)]}}
. It is also shown, that if in addition
|\Theta|
is bounded and
\text{Var}[f(X)]
is bounded away from zero, then

\frac{{\left\|{\bf{B}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{n^{2}\text{Var}[f(X)]}}}\to 0
and
\frac{{\left\|{\bf{C}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{\{n\text{Var}[f(X)]\}^{2}}}}\to 0.

Proposition 4 in
Livne et al. 2021
shows that

\frac{{\left\|{\bf{B}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{n^{2}\text{Var}[f(X)]}}}\to 0
and
\frac{{\left\|{\bf{C}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}}{{{\{n\text{Var}[f(X)]\}^{2}}}}\to 0

hold also when
|\Theta|
is unbounded, but with additional conditions on linearity and independence of the covariates. It is also shown there that under those assumptions,
\frac{{{{\left\|\theta_{b}\right\|}^{2}}}}{{\text{Var}[f(X)]}}
is bounded.
In simulations, which are not presented here, we observed that these conditions also hold for various non-linear models.

Notice that the zero-estimator
Z_{g}
, which is not based on any covariate-selection procedure, is just a special case of
Z_{h}
when
\delta
selects all the
p
covariates, i.e.,
{\bf{S}}_{\delta}=\{1,\dots p\}
.
Hence, if the conditions of Proposition
3.4
hold, then
\sqrt{n}\left[T_{\hat{g}}-T_{g}\right]\overset{p}{\rightarrow}~0,

where
T_{\hat{g}}
and
T_{g}
are given in
(
3.16
) and (
3.14
), respectively.

3.5
Simulations Results

In this section, we illustrate the performance of the proposed estimators using simulations. Specifically, we compare the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
and the improved estimators
T_{\hat{g}}
and
T_{\hat{h}}
which are defined in (
2.5
), (
3.16
), and Algorithm
5
, respectively. The code for reproducing the results of this section and the next section
(
2.6
)
is available at
https://t.ly/dwJg
.

For demonstration purposes, we consider a setting in which
K
entries of the vector
\beta
are
relatively large (in absolute value), and all other entries are small.
The proportion of the signal in those
K
entries is defined as the
sparsity level
of the vector
\beta
. Next, we study different sparsity levels by defining the following non-linear model,

{Y_{i}}={\gamma_{L}}\sum\limits_{j\in\Theta}{\left[{{X_{ij}}+\sin\left({{X_{ij}}}\right)}\right]}+{\gamma_{S}}\sum\limits_{j\notin\Theta}{\left[{{X_{ij}}+\sin\left({{X_{ij}}}\right)}\right]}+{\xi_{i}},\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pti=1,\dots,n,

(3.21)

where

{\gamma_{L}}\equiv{\left\{{\frac{{\eta{\tau^{2}}}}{{k{{\left({1+E\left[{X\sin\left(X\right)}\right]}\right)}^{2}}}}}\right\}^{1/2}}

,

{\gamma_{S}}\equiv{\left\{{\frac{{{\tau^{2}}\left({1-\eta}\right)}}{{\left({p-k}\right){{\left({1+E\left[{X\sin\left(X\right)}\right]}\right)}^{2}}}}}\right\}^{1/2}}
, and
\Theta
is the set of the largest
K
entries of the vector
\beta.

The model has two parameters,
\tau^{2}
and
\eta
, that vary across the different simulation scenarios.
The covariates were generated from the centered exponential distribution, i.e.,
X_{ij}\overset{iid}{\sim}\text{Exp}(1)-1,

i=1,\dots,n
,
j=1,\dots,p
. The noise level
\xi_{i}
was generated from the standard normal distribution.
One can verify that under the above model
\beta_{j}^{2}=\frac{{\eta{\tau^{2}}}}{K}
for
j\in\Theta
, and that

\beta_{j}^{2}=\frac{{{\tau^{2}}\left({1-\eta}\right)}}{{\left({p-K}\right)}}\,
for
j\notin\Theta.

Define
\tau_{\Theta}^{2}\equiv\sum\limits_{j\in\Theta}{\beta_{j}^{2}}
. From the above definitions, it follows that
\eta=~\tau_{\Theta}^{2}/{\tau^{2}}
.
The parameter
\eta
is the proportion of signal that is captured by the set
\Theta,
which is the sparsity level as defined above. The case of full sparsity, where the entire signal level
\tau^{2}
comes only from the set
\Theta
, corresponds to
\eta=1
, and is not assumed here.

We fix
n=p=300
and
K=6
.
For each combination of the parameters
\tau^{2}\in\{1,2\}
and
\eta\in\{{0.1,0.3,0.5,0.7,0.9}\},
we generated 100 independent datasets from model (
3.21
) and estimated
\tau^{2}
using the different estimators.
The covariate-selection procedure
\delta
that was used in the estimator
T_{\hat{h}}
is defined in Remark
2.9
in the Appendix.

Figure
4
plots the RMSE of each estimator as a function of the sparsity level
\eta
and the signal level
\tau^{2}.
It is demonstrated that
the estimators
T_{\hat{g}}
and
T_{\hat{h}}
improve (i.e., lower or equal RMSE) the naive estimator
in all settings.
The improved estimators are complementary to each other, i.e.,
for small values of
\eta
the estimator
T_{\hat{g}}
performs better than
T_{\hat{h}},
and the opposite occurs for large values of
\eta.

This is expected since
when the sparsity level
\eta
is small,
the improvement of
T_{\hat{h}}
is smaller as it ignores much of the signal that lies outside of the set
\Theta.

On the other hand, when a
large portion of the signal
\tau^{2}
is captured by only the few covariates in
\Theta
, it is sufficient to make use of only these covariates in the zero-estimator term, and the improvement of
T_{\hat{h}}
is greater.

Table
1
shows the RMSE, bias, standard error, and the relative improvement, for the different estimators.
It can be observed that the degree of improvements depends on the sparsity level of the data
\eta
.
For example, when
\tau^{2}=1
and sparsity level is low (
\eta=0.1
), the estimator
T_{\hat{g}}
improves the naive estimator by
11\%
, while the estimator
T_{\hat{h}}
presents a similar performance to the naive estimator.
On the other hand, when the sparsity level is high (
\eta=0.9
), the estimator
T_{\hat{h}}
improves the naive by
11\%
, while
T_{\hat{g}}
presents a similar performance to the naive estimator, as expected.
Notice that when
\tau^{2}=2
these improvements are even more substantial.

Root mean square error (RMSE) for the proposed estimators. The x-axis stands for the sparsity level
\eta
.

) was calculated using the delta method.

In the following we consider the case that the distribution of the covariates is only partially known.
It is assumed that a large amount of unlabeled data is available and therefore the distribution of the covariates can be estimated by using this data.
Specifically, we assume that in addition to the label data
(X_{1},Y_{1}),...,(X_{n},Y_{n}),
we have access to additional
N
i.i.d observations
X_{n+1},...,X_{n+N}.
However, the responses
Y_{n+1},...,Y_{n+N}
are missing.
Additionally, a specific correlation structure is assumed.
Namely, we consider the case that
\Sigma_{X}
is a
b
-banded matrix, i.e.,
[\Sigma_{X}]_{jj^{\prime}}=0
for all
|j-j^{\prime}|>b,
where
b
is referred as the bandwidth of the matrix diagonal. This assumption is common in GWAS; see e.g.,
Bickel and Levina 2008
and
Cai et al. 2010
.
Unlike in the previous simulation setting, here
\mu
and
{\Sigma_{X}}
are estimated using the unlabeled data, namely,
\hat{\mu}\equiv\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i={n+1}}^{n+N}X_{i}
and

[{\hat{\Sigma}_{X}}]_{jj^{\prime}}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+N}(X_{i,j}-\hat{\mu}_{j})(X_{i,j^{\prime}}-\hat{\mu}_{j^{\prime}})&|j-j^{\prime}|\leq b\\
0&|j-j^{\prime}|>b\end{array}\right..

We then apply the linear transformation,

X\mapsto{\hat{\Sigma}_{X}}^{-1/2}(X-\hat{\mu}),

which corresponds the transformation shown in Section
3.2
.
We fixed
b=5
and repeated the simulation study above for different values of
N.

Notice that under the above setting, the true
\Sigma_{X}
is the identity, and in particular it is indeed a
5
-banded matrix.

Table
6
is similar to Table
1
but includes different values of
N
rather than different values of
\eta.

For brevity, we present only the scenario of

\tau^{2}=2
and
\eta=0.9
but the results for other scenarios are similar.
It can be observed from the table that for large values of
N
the results are fairly similar to those in Table
1
.

3.6
Generalization to Other Estimators

The suggested methodology in this paper is not limited to improving only the naive estimator, but can also be generalized to other estimators. As before, the key idea is to use a zero-estimator that is correlated with an initial estimator of
\tau^{2}
in order to reduce its variance. Unlike the naive estimator
\hat{\tau}^{2}
, which has a closed-form expression, other estimators, such as the EigenPrism estimator
(
Janson et al. 2017
)
, are computed numerically by solving a convex optimization problem. For a given zero-estimator, this makes the task of estimating the optimal-coefficient
c^{*}
more challenging than before.
To overcome this challenge, we approximate the optimal coefficient
c^{*}
using bootstrap samples.
This is described in the following algorithm.

Input:

A dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
, an estimation procedure
\tilde{\tau}^{2},
and a covariate-selection procedure
\delta
.

1.

Apply the procedure
\delta
to the dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
to obtain
{{\bf{S}}_{\delta}}.

2.

Apply the procedure
\tilde{\tau}^{2}
to the dataset
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
.

3.

Calculate the zero-estimator

{Z_{h}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{h\left({{X_{i}}}\right)}
,
where
h\left({{X_{i}}}\right)=\sum\limits_{j<j^{\prime}\in{{\bf{S}}_{\delta}}}{{X_{ij}}{X_{ij^{\prime}}}}.

4.

Bootstrap step:

•

Sample
n
observations at random from
\left({{{\bf{X}}_{n\times p}},{Y_{n\times 1}}}\right)
, with replacement, to obtain a bootstrap dataset.

•

Repeat steps 2 and 3 based on the bootstrap dataset.

The bootstrap step is repeated
M
times in order to produce

(\tilde{\tau}^{2})^{*1},...,(\tilde{\tau}^{2})^{*M}
and

Z_{h}^{*1},...,Z_{h}^{*M}.

5.

Approximate the coefficient
\tilde{c}_{h}^{*}=\frac{{\widehat{\text{Cov}\left({\tilde{\tau}^{2},{Z_{h}}}\right)}}}{{\text{Var}\left({{Z_{h}}}\right)}}

where
\widehat{{\text{Cov}}\left(\cdot\right)}
denotes the empirical covariance from the bootstrap samples.

Output
:
Return the empirical estimator

T_{\tilde{h}}\equiv\tilde{\tau}^{2}-\tilde{c}_{h}^{*}Z_{h}.

Algorithm 6

Empirical Estimators

In the special case when
\delta
selects all the
p
covariates, i.e.,
{\bf{S}}_{\delta}=\{1,\dots p\}
, we use the notations
Z_{g}
and
\tilde{c}_{g}^{*}
rather than
Z_{h}
and
\tilde{c}_{h}^{*}
, respectively, i.e.,
T_{\tilde{g}}\equiv\tilde{\tau}^{2}-\tilde{c}_{g}^{*}Z_{g}.

We illustrate the improvement obtained by Algorithm
6
by choosing
\tilde{\tau}^{2}
to be the EigenPrism procedure
(
Janson et al. 2017
)
, but other estimators can be used as well.
We consider the same setting as in Section
2.5
. The number of bootstrap samples is
M=100.

The simulation results appear in Table
7
and Figure
5
.
Both estimators
T_{\tilde{h}}
and
T_{\tilde{g}}
show an improvement over the EigenPrism estimator
\tilde{\tau}^{2}.

The results here are fairly similar to the results shown for the naive estimator in Section
2.5
, with just a smaller degree of improvement. As before,
the improved estimators
T_{\tilde{h}}
and
T_{\tilde{g}}
are complementary to each other, i.e.,
for small values of
\eta
the estimator
T_{\tilde{g}}
performs better than
T_{\tilde{h}},
and the opposite occurs for large values of
\eta.

Figure 5:

Root mean square error (RMSE) for the proposed estimators. The x-axis stands for the sparsity level
\eta
.
